This week, senior writer Kate Knibbs joins Brian Barrett, Zoë Schiffer, and Leah Feiger to discuss the two incidents that have been making noise in prediction markets: George Santos getting lifetime ban from Kalshi and a Google engineer’s Polymarket insider-trading case. Brian Barrett: It reminds me a lot of, or at least rhymes with geofence warrants, which for those who don't know, a geofence warrant is where law enforcement could go to Google and say, ""Hey, I want to know everything about all the cell phone data, location data for anyone who was near a particular place during a particular time."" This was, I think used, if I remember correctly, pretty extensively around the January 6 riots. They found a lot of people through these geofence warrants because they were able to say, ""Hey, Google, who had a phone inside the Capitol at this time who was not a member of Congress?"" The Supreme Court just a couple months ago ruled that those kind of geofence warrants do count as a search under the Fourth Amendment. So, you need a reasonable search requirement, it's a higher bar to actually conduct these things than there was because they were just going willy-nilly. 2,2026-09-03,"Wooden cube block building the word HR at the center on grid line note book with random other blocks in the background, represent Human Resource department

Rippling bundles HR with IT and finance, but mid-market HR teams often pay for modules they never use. This roundup ranks 12 alternatives that start from the people side: HiBob (Bob) leads for HR-first mid-market teams, Paylocity and BambooHR cover core HR and payroll, Deel and Remote handle global hiring, and Workday and ADP serve larger enterprises. Each profile includes G2 ratings, reviewer complaints, and pricing structures.
Rippling sells one bundle for HR, IT, and finance, and the pitch lands well until an HR team reads the invoice. The base platform carries a low sticker price, then payroll, benefits, device management, and app provisioning each arrive as separate modules with their own per-employee fees. For a people team that wants a strong HR system and little else, that structure means paying for infrastructure it will never touch. Searches for rippling alternatives climb every quarter for that reason: buyers want the parts that serve employees without the IT-admin machinery wrapped around them.
This roundup looks at 12 platforms that give HR leaders a people-first system, ranked with mid-market companies in mind. Each entry covers who the tool fits, what it does well, where reviewers push back, and how pricing works, so you can build a shortlist with clear eyes rather than a demo-day hunch.
Rippling launched in 2016 as a way to run HR, payroll, IT, and spend management from one place. The device management piece sets it apart: the platform provisions laptops, controls app access, and enforces security policies, which no traditional HR system does. For a lean startup where one person handles onboarding and IT, that combination saves real time.
The trouble starts when the buyer is an HR team, not an ops generalist. Rippling prices by module, so the workforce directory is one line item and payroll, benefits, and IT each add another. Published pricing analyses put the core module around $8 per employee per month, with the working total landing between $25 and $50 once a team switches on payroll and the other pieces it needs. A people team ends up funding device management and app provisioning it has no plans to use.
Depth creates a second issue. Reviewers on G2 flag a steep learning curve and note that the breadth of settings takes dedicated effort to configure. The platform’s newer modules, including recruiting and benefits enrollment, draw complaints about missing features next to specialist tools. Support routes through admins, so an employee with a payroll question has to file it through HR rather than reaching Rippling.
Rippling remains a capable product. The mismatch is one of shape: it bundles IT with HR for buyers who want a people system on its own terms. The alternatives below start from that people side.
Here is how the 12 platforms compare on fit, strengths, and pricing before the deeper profiles below.
Best for: Mid-market companies that want a people-first HR platform without the IT-admin weight
Bob, the platform built by HiBob, treats HR as the main event rather than a companion to device management. Bob runs on a required foundation called Bob Core, which unifies people data, self-service, workflows, and analytics in one place, then lets companies add suites for talent, payroll, and planning as they grow. Where Rippling wraps HR inside an IT-and-finance bundle, Bob keeps the people experience at the center, which suits HR leaders who want engagement and culture tools in the core product rather than bolted on later.
The platform earns attention for how it feels to use. The interface is consumer-grade, the kind employees open without a training session. Recognition and performance reviews sit inside the same system that holds records and payroll, so managers act on sentiment data without exporting it to a separate engagement tool. For a growing mid-market company, that consolidation replaces the point-tool stack many teams outgrow.
Pricing is the honest catch. HiBob keeps rates off the public page, so the entry point is a scoped quote rather than a single published module fee. Teams that want a public per-seat number will need a sales conversation first. In return they get a modern people platform that goes live in weeks and carries 4.5 out of 5 across a base of more than 1,800 G2 reviews.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Not published; HiBob provides a custom quote after a demo, and every plan starts with Bob Core.
Best for: Mid-market companies that want HR, payroll, and finance in a single system
Paylocity brings payroll, benefits, HR, and an engagement layer together for mid-market employers, and it leans harder on culture tools than most payroll-rooted platforms. Its Community feature works as an internal social hub with chat and video, and its recognition tools give employees a way to reward peers, which appeals to companies that want engagement built in rather than sourced from a separate vendor. Against Rippling, the pitch is HR-specific automation and business-user reporting instead of IT provisioning.
The reviews temper the story. Across a base of more than 5,300 G2 ratings, Paylocity sits at 4.4 out of 5, and the most common complaints point at support responsiveness and a setup that takes patience to learn. Some reviewers also flag features they expected to find missing from certain tiers, so a detailed scoping conversation matters before signing.
Paylocity fits an HR team that wants payroll and engagement under one roof and can absorb a learning curve during rollout. Pricing follows a custom-quote model, so budgeting means a sales call.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; Paylocity doesn’t publish standard rates.
Best for: US small businesses that want payroll to run itself
Gusto made its name on payroll a founder can run without an HR background. Automatic tax filing at the federal, state, and local level comes included, and its AutoPilot setting runs each cycle on schedule, which removes the manual step that trips up small teams. For a US company under about 150 employees, Gusto covers payroll and light HR with an approachable interface.
Scale is where it strains. Reviewers note that the platform fits smaller headcounts better than growing ones, and its 4.6 average across a base of more than 10,000 G2 reviews sits alongside recurring complaints about support wait times and payroll or login problems. A company expecting to double headcount may outrun Gusto’s depth in workforce planning and advanced HR.
Gusto suits a small business that wants clean payroll today, with the caveat that heavier HR needs push buyers toward a broader platform later.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Simple plan from $49 per month plus $6 per employee; Plus plan from $80 per month plus $12 per employee; Premium at custom pricing.
Best for: Small and growing teams that want approachable core HR
BambooHR helped define the approachable HR system for companies leaving spreadsheets behind. It centralizes employee records, runs onboarding with e-signatures and checklists, and gives staff a clean self-service portal with visual org charts, all without demanding IT expertise to manage. Set against Rippling’s automation depth, BambooHR trades breadth for a gentler learning curve.
The ceiling shows up in customization. Reviewers across a base of more than 5,000 G2 ratings give it 4.4 out of 5 while noting limited options for complex workflows and reporting that struggles with deep custom analytics. Companies with intricate approval chains or heavy analytics needs tend to hit those walls as they scale past a few hundred employees.
BambooHR works best for a team that values ease of use over configurability and wants core HR running fast. Pricing is quote-based.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; BambooHR doesn’t list public rates.
Best for: HR teams hiring employees and contractors across borders
Deel earns its place on an HR shortlist when hiring crosses borders. The platform acts as an Employer of Record in 150+ countries, which lets a company put someone on payroll abroad without opening a local entity, and it handles contractor payments in dozens of currencies. For an HR leader at a mid-market company adding a first overseas hire, Deel removes the legal setup that would otherwise stall the plan.
The trade-offs sit on the money side. Deel holds a strong 4.7 out of 5 across a base of more than 6,500 G2 reviews, yet the recurring complaints cluster around payment reliability and cost, with some reviewers also citing processing delays. Deel stays narrow by design: it isn’t a domestic HRIS, so a company using it for global hires still needs a core HR system for everyone at home.
Deel fits HR teams whose main gap is international employment rather than day-to-day people management. It pairs with a domestic platform rather than replacing one.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Contractor management from about $49 per contractor per month; EOR pricing quote-based.
Best for: Large enterprises with complex, multinational HR and finance
Workday plays at the top of the market, built for enterprises that run HR and finance on one data model across many countries. Its strength is depth: workforce planning and multi-entity financials sit in a single system that global companies use to steer thousands of employees. For an organization at that scale, Workday’s configurability is the draw.
That power comes with weight. Workday holds 4.1 on G2, where reviewers flag a demanding learning curve and an interface that lags more modern tools. Implementations often run several months and lean on dedicated consultants, and many enterprises keep full-time administrators to maintain the system. A mid-market HR team tends to find that overhead hard to justify.
Workday suits large, complex organizations with the budget and staff to run it. Growing companies often find it heavier than the job requires. Pricing is custom and enterprise-scaled.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; enterprise-scaled and not published.
Best for: Mid-market and larger firms that want established payroll and compliance
ADP has run payroll since 1949, and ADP Workforce Now is its platform for mid-market employers. The reliability argument is real: certified payroll professionals and deep compliance coverage back a service model that large organizations trust to get pay right at scale. For a company that prizes a proven payroll engine over modern design, ADP delivers.
Age shows in the experience: reviewers complain about a dated interface and menus that bury simple tasks. Its 4.2 on G2 sits alongside notes about support quality and limited customization. HR teams that expect a consumer-grade interface often find the platform a step behind newer systems.
ADP Workforce Now fits established firms that want payroll muscle and can live with older UX. Pricing is quote-based at every tier.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; ADP doesn’t publish standard rates.
Best for: Established small businesses that want payroll with hands-on support
Paychex has served US payroll since 1971 and counts hundreds of thousands of clients, with dedicated payroll specialists as its calling card. Companies that would rather call a person than log into a self-service tool value that human model, and its PEO option lets small businesses hand off HR administration wholesale. Against Rippling’s automation-first approach, Paychex sells relationship and reliability.
The friction is service consistency and cost. Paychex holds 4.1 on G2, where reviewers report support responsiveness problems and billing frustrations, and some note that tax filing and accounting integrations carry extra fees that competitors fold into base pricing. Buyers watching total cost should price the add-ons before committing.
Paychex fits an established small business that wants payroll handled by a named contact. Growing HR teams that want a modern people platform tend to look elsewhere. Pricing is quote-based.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; Paychex prices by service tier.
Best for: European SMBs consolidating core HR and recruiting
Personio grew up serving small and mid-sized companies across Europe, and it centralizes core HR and recruiting in one system tuned to regional compliance, with absence management built in. For a company whose workforce is concentrated in Europe, that local focus is a genuine advantage, and the applicant tracking tools give lean teams a recruiting workflow without a second vendor.
Reach and depth are the limits. Personio holds 4.4 out of 5 across a base of more than 800 G2 reviews, where reviewers cite feature gaps and shallow customization, and its strength thins outside Europe, where localization and advanced capabilities like workforce planning lag. A US-headquartered mid-market company tends to find the fit weaker than a European-first platform promises.
Personio suits European SMBs that want core HR and hiring together. Companies with heavy North American operations or advanced planning needs should weigh that gap. Pricing is quote-based.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; Personio doesn’t list public rates.
Best for: Small businesses that want a PEO to carry payroll and benefits
Justworks operates a professional employer organization model and becomes the co-employer that handles payroll, benefits, and compliance so a small business doesn’t have to staff those functions. The appeal is access and simplicity: through the PEO model, small teams reach benefits plans often reserved for larger employers, and the interface stays clean enough for owners without HR training.
The constraints match the model. Justworks holds 4.6 out of 5 across a base of more than 1,100 G2 reviews, with reviewers flagging missing features and integration limits as the company grows, alongside inconsistent support. A business that needs deep HR configurability or advanced talent tools tends to feel boxed in by the PEO structure.
Justworks fits a small business that wants payroll and benefits carried for it rather than a full people platform to run. Pricing is quote-based per employee.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; priced per employee under the PEO model.
Best for: Mid-sized teams that want employees to drive their own payroll
Paycom runs payroll and HR from one shared database, and its signature idea is Beti, an employee-guided payroll tool that has staff review and fix their own paychecks before submission. That shift moves verification to the person who knows their pay best and cuts correction cycles for HR. For a mid-sized company that wants payroll accuracy with less admin chasing, the model has real appeal.
Adoption is the hurdle. Paycom holds 4.6 out of 5 across a base of more than 7,000 G2 reviews, yet reviewers note a steep learning curve, plus features that feel thin next to specialist tools, alongside support gaps. The single-database design also discourages outside integrations, so teams with a varied tech stack may face more manual data entry.
Paycom fits mid-sized organizations ready to push payroll tasks to employees and invest in onboarding the system. Pricing is quote-based.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; Paycom prices by configuration.
Best for: Distributed teams hiring worldwide through one Employer of Record
Remote closes the list as the global-hiring option for HR teams that run distributed workforces. Like Deel, it acts as an Employer of Record and manages contractors, letting a company employ people in countries where it holds no entity, with an emphasis on owning local infrastructure and protecting intellectual property across borders. For an HR leader building a remote-first team, it centralizes global employment in one place.
The caveats track its category. Remote holds 4.5 out of 5 across a base of more than 4,800 G2 reviews, where reviewers report support gaps and processing delays, with occasional payment issues. It also isn’t a domestic HR system, so a company still needs a core people platform for the wider organization.
Remote suits distributed teams whose priority is compliant global hiring rather than everyday HR administration. It complements a core HRIS instead of standing in for one. Pricing is quote-based.
Highlight features:
What it costs: Custom quote; Remote prices per worker and service.
Shortlisting gets easier once you name what you need before the demos start. These factors separate a genuine fit from a slick sales deck.
Match the tool to your real gap
Name the one or two problems you’re solving before you scope features. A team that needs engagement and performance shouldn’t pay for IT device management, and a company hiring abroad shouldn’t buy a domestic-only HRIS. The narrower your gap, the clearer the shortlist.
Weigh the employee experience
The best HR platform is the one employees open without help. Test the self-service flow and the mobile experience, then watch how a manager acts on survey results. If people struggle to request time off or read a pay stub, HR inherits the questions.
Price the whole thing, not the sticker
Module pricing hides the real number. Ask what the running total looks like once a company adds the tiers it needs, and get implementation fees and renewal increases in writing. A low base rate means little if the working configuration costs three times as much.
Check implementation and support
Ask how long rollout takes and who owns it. Some platforms go live in weeks; enterprise systems can run months and need consultants. Confirm whether employees can reach support or everything routes through an admin.
Test reporting against a real question
Bring a report you lean on, like headcount by department or a turnover trend, and ask the vendor to build it live. Strong analytics answer in minutes without an export to a spreadsheet.
The through-line across these Rippling competitors is shape. Rippling bundles IT and finance around HR and prices each piece on its own; the alternatives here let you buy the people system without the infrastructure you won’t run. For most mid-market HR teams, that framing settles the choice.
Bob tops the list because it answers the exact reason buyers leave Rippling. It puts the people experience in the core rather than treating engagement as an add-on, and it scales from a growing team to a multinational without the IT-admin weight, going live in weeks rather than months. The honest trade is pricing quoted after a demo instead of listed on a page, plus a required Bob Core foundation. For a company that wants HR to feel modern and its data to live in one place, Bob is the strongest starting point among 2026 Rippling alternatives.
What are the best alternatives to Rippling?
The strongest alternatives depend on the gap you’re filling. For an HR-first platform, Paylocity and BambooHR compete on core HR and payroll, while global hiring points toward Deel or Remote. Enterprises with heavy finance needs weigh Workday, and smaller teams lean on Gusto for payroll.
Who is Rippling’s biggest competitor?
No single name owns the title, since Rippling spans HR, IT, and payroll. On the HR side, Paylocity and BambooHR come up most in buyer comparisons, with ADP close behind. For teams leaving Rippling because they want people-first HR rather than an IT bundle, Bob is a frequent pick.
Why do HR teams leave Rippling?
Two reasons dominate. Rippling prices each module on its own, so an HR team ends up paying for IT and finance tools it won’t use, and reviewers point to a learning curve that takes effort to climb. Teams that want HR without the IT-admin layer tend to start looking for alternatives.
Is there a free Rippling alternative?
Most established HR platforms, Rippling included, don’t offer a genuine free tier; they price by quote or per employee. A few lightweight tools advertise free plans, though they cap features fast. For a growing mid-market company, the real question is value at scale, since a cheap tool you outgrow in a year costs more to replace.
Why is Rippling better than Workday?
For most small and mid-sized companies, Rippling is easier to deploy and lighter to run than Workday, which targets large enterprises and often needs months of implementation and dedicated administrators. Workday wins on enterprise depth in finance and planning. Rippling wins on speed and modern IT automation for smaller teams.
What are the top HR systems for mid-market companies?
Mid-market buyers weigh core HR, payroll, engagement, and analytics in one place. Paylocity and BambooHR show up on most lists, with Personio common in Europe, and Bob ranks near the top for teams that want a modern people experience with workforce planning and native US payroll in the same system. The right pick comes down to whether engagement and culture tools matter as much as payroll.
Rippling vs Deel: which fits an HR team better?
They solve different problems. Deel is built for hiring employees and contractors across borders through an Employer of Record, while Rippling is a broader HR-IT platform with global payroll added on. An HR team whose main gap is international hiring leans Deel; one that wants a domestic people platform looks at options like Bob or Paylocity instead.
How much does Rippling cost compared to alternatives?
Published analyses put Rippling’s core module around $8 per employee per month, with the working total between $25 and $50 once a company adds payroll and the other modules it needs. Most alternatives use custom quotes rather than public rates, so a direct comparison means requesting numbers for the exact configuration you need.
What’s the best HR-first alternative to Rippling?
For teams that want HR at the center rather than wrapped around IT, Bob stands out. It keeps engagement and people analytics in the core product, goes live in weeks, and scales from a growing company to a multinational. The catch is quote-based pricing and a required Bob Core foundation, so budget a demo into your evaluation.

7,2026-09-03,"I still remember the first time I set foot on Market Street. I was a freshman in college, and big cities were still a very new thing to me. It felt like I was swimming through an ocean of foot traffic; the towering shops and hotels seemed like they were built by giants. 
That Market Street is gone now. In its place is a giant empty field of asphalt in the center of San Francisco — a desolate gray waste baking in the sun, empty except for the occasional bus and a tiny smattering of bicycles.
On either side of this yawning wasteland are giant sidewalks with only a few pedestrians, boarded-up stores, and the occasional small, sad retail chain. At midday on a Monday, what was once the bustling commercial heart of San Francisco feels abandoned. 
What happened? In fact, Market Street was destroyed by several things:
The city’s decision to close the street to cars (but not buses), enacted in 2019
The crime wave of the early 2020s
The pandemic, and the shift to remote work
The second and third of these, together, are known as the “Doom Loop”. It’s been talked about a lot, so let’s just cover it briefly. 
The pandemic, and the economic and social changes it brought, came out of the blue. But San Francisco was particularly vulnerable to the shock. As in many American cities, 20th century urban planning and zoning rules concentrated office buildings in the downtown area and put housing farther out. This meant that once people stopped going downtown for work, the neighborhood was just a lot more empty, because almost no one lives there. This is in contrast to big cities in Europe and Asia, where downtown neighborhoods are typically mixed-use. (SF also had a stronger shift to remote work, because of the heavy presence of the tech industry.)
The crime wave was another shock. Although SF escaped the destructive rioting that hit other West Coast cities like Seattle, Portland, and Los Angeles in 2020, it had plenty of looting in downtown areas — especially around Union Square and the San Francisco Centre mall on Market Street. Even after the pandemic ended, San Francisco suffered especially hard from the statewide wave of “smash-and-grab” store thefts. It got so bad that American Eagle actually sued the San Francisco Centre for being negligent toward crime, reporting over 100 serious incidents of violence and harassment in its downtown store.
Crime and emptiness fed on each other. Less foot traffic meant fewer “eyes on the street” to discourage property crime. Property crime and reduced foot traffic forced stores to close. Fewer stores meant less foot traffic, and so on. The collapse of downtown culminated in January 2026 with the closure of the San Francisco Centre, the iconic mall that had formed the commercial heart of Market Street, but which had been gutted by the Doom Loop. It now stands as a looming, empty, boarded-up structure dominating the area around the Powell Street train station:
The intersection of Powell Street and Market Street, once bustling and alive with tourists, shoppers, and street vendors, is now virtually empty, except for a forlorn line of people waiting to get on the iconic streetcar:
But recent years have seen the first glints of recovery from the Doom Loop. For one thing, crime has begun to fall. 2025 alone saw a 27% decrease in property crime, including a 29% decrease in burglaries, a 22% decrease in larceny (including retail theft), and a 24% decrease in robbery. 2026 has seen further drops. The reasons for this are interesting, but I’ll go into them another time. Suffice it to say that this has made downtown SF feel much safer and more orderly. The stereotype of SF as being overrun by drug addicts and dangerous vagrants still holds true of a few areas of the city, but from Powell Street to the bay, the city no longer feels like a post-apocalyptic movie. 
Meanwhile, the business district is showing a few sluggish signs of life. The AI boom is causing a (modest) rebound in the demand for office space. Some office buildings are being converted to housing, raising hopes that SF’s downtown can finally become a mixed-use neighborhood. A concerted effort to revive Union Square after the devastation of 2020 is beginning to bear fruit. And both companies and the SF city government are mandating return-to-office policies. 
And yet Market Street remains a dead zone. Weekday foot traffic is still down by half compared to 2019:
The wounds inflicted by the Doom Loop will take lots more time and effort to heal. Retail businesses have to be encouraged to open. Defunct buildings have to be torn down and rebuilt. Crime has to be reduced even further, and so on. This will all take years.
But there’s one other factor contributing to the death of San Francisco’s most important street, and it began before the pandemic: The ban on cars. 
In recent years, a number of cities around the world have experimented with car-free zones and traffic reduction policies in their downtown areas. The most famous of these is Paris, with its Limited Traffic Zone, its expansion of pedestrian malls, and its road closures during Sundays and holidays. Shortly before the pandemic, San Francisco decided to enact a plan its leaders thought was in the same spirit: closing downtown Market Street to all car traffic:
But although Market Street was closed to cars, it wasn’t closed to buses, delivery trucks, and licensed taxis. This was in keeping with the general “transit good, cars bad” thinking of modern progressive urbanism. But a bus will kill a pedestrian just as easily as a car will, which is why Market Street remains empty of pedestrian traffic. This mathematically guaranteed that Market Street would become a more empty place: Cars were subtracted, but pedestrians weren’t added. 
The direct cost of this policy is pretty obvious. When people can’t park in a parking garage on Market, and when Uber and Lyft can’t pick people up and drop people off on the street, it makes businesses on the street less attractive to visit. Sure, people could bike or take the train, but not everyone wants to do that; instead, they’ll just go to restaurants or stores in other areas. In addition, restaurants on Market Street now can’t deliver food, cutting them off from an important source of revenue. That probably helps explain why Market has suffered more from the Doom Loop than other areas. 
But what did SF gain by deleting cars from Market Street and adding nothing at all in their place? Cars create a little bit of pollution (though modern cars are very clean), so that was removed. Pedestrians who run off the sidewalk and into the street are somewhat less in danger of being killed (though still in danger). These are meager benefits indeed. 
What about bike traffic? Presumably, reduced danger of being hit by a car would allow more cyclists to ride down Market Street. But in fact, bike traffic on the street is considerably lower than it was pre-pandemic:
A lot of that is the Doom Loop — there are simply fewer stores for cyclists to visit on Market Street, so there’s less reason for them to go there. But much of downtown hasn’t collapsed the way Market Street has, and presumably cyclists could use the street as a way to get to places like the Embarcadero, Chinatown, etc. They’re not doing it. Cyclists are a relatively rare sight on Market these days.
One reason might be that although the city planned to make the car-free stretch of Market more bike-friendly, it never really followed up on those plans. So it’s still pretty dangerous to ride a bike or scooter down the street — you can get caught in the train tracks, and you can get hit by a bus or truck. 
In fact, this sloppy implementation is a general pattern with the Market Street closure. Josh Koehn had a great article in 2023 about how the plan degenerated from its initial form:
A $600 million capital project called Better Market Street promised to create a futuristic boulevard that would safely buffer bicycles and scooters on elevated sidewalk lanes, separating the little wheels from rapid bus lines, vintage streetcars and pedestrians…The new vision for Market Street, advocates said, would create a safer, more vibrant multimodal avenue…More than a decade of planning went into alterations of Market Street…
But funds for anything beyond modest improvements along a three-block stretch of Market Street between Fifth and Eighth streets…have evaporated…The cost of Better Market Street, which soared above a billion dollars before being reined in to a still-staggering $604 million, is almost double the amount spent on the tormented Van Ness Avenue redesign…
City officials enraged safe streets advocates when they announced in late 2020 that the elevated bike lanes on sidewalks would be scrapped.
SF’s lack of state capacity had a lot to do with this; constant cost overruns inevitably require ambitious projects to be scaled back. The pandemic also obviously got in the way. But part of what killed the vision for Market Street is simply that the vision itself was never very good. 
If you look at Paris, it’s immediately clear how that city’s car-limitation measures were designed to create spaces for pedestrians. In certain areas like the banks of the Seine, Paris didn’t just ban cars; it also banned buses and trucks, so that people could walk safely. Paris also reserved much larger areas for pedestrians on certain days. 
But Market Street has no more pedestrian areas than it did in 2019. You can still only walk on the sidewalk. If the entire street were reserved for foot traffic during certain times — like SF does with Valencia Street, for example — then these periodic temporary pedestrianizations could become an event that regularly draws lots of people to the area. This could include farmers’ markets, street concerts, and other fun events. Those would give people a reason to walk around Market Street even before new businesses opened. 
Alternatively, if SF permanently closed off some portion of the street to buses and trucks, that stretch of road could become a pedestrian public square, similar to the center of Times Square:
Instead of thinking about how to create spaces that people would actually want to walk around in, the SF government — and the many activist groups and nonprofits that influence its policy — appear to have adhered to the simple rule of “bikes and buses good, cars bad”. 
This is a way of thinking centered around modes of transit rather than public space. It implicitly conceives of areas like Market Street as thoroughfares to take people from one place to another, rather than as actual destinations. Market Street’s car closure is all about forcing people to go down Market Street in a different way, rather than about encouraging people to go to Market Street.
There’s something inescapably and depressingly suburban about this way of thinking. In the suburbs, life is lived point-to-point — you go from house to strip-mall to restaurant to office without thinking much about the places you traverse in between. In a suburb, the mode of transportation — how you get from Point A to Point B — is the important question. 
Americans are a suburban people; even many of our urban areas look like suburbs in other countries. So perhaps it’s inevitable that the urbanists who closed Market Street off to car traffic would think entirely about modes of transportation and ignore the vibrant city center whose destruction they exacerbated. Instead of Jane Jacobs, they ended up just being the Robert Moses of buses. 
Again, careful attention to car limitations in cities like Paris, where people are used to occupying urban places instead of simply whizzing through them, would have proven enlightening. Paris’s Limited Traffic Zone doesn’t ban cars in downtown areas; it merely bans cars from transiting through those areas. People who stay within the area can still drive. The point is to make downtown spaces into places people occupy rather than places people simply go through. What San Francisco did to Market Street was, in many ways, the exact opposite.
In lieu of turning Market Street into a pedestrian paradise, SF’s best move is probably just to allow private cars again. It’s not the optimal way to create more foot traffic to nourish retail businesses, but it’s the quickest and easiest. In fact, Mayor Daniel Lurie has made moves in this direction, allowing certain Uber, Lyft, and Waymo cars to operate on the street. But they might as well simply go all the way. Best to simply bury the era when Market Street was a large empty scar commemorating the decline of San Francisco; if San Francisco can’t be Paris, at least it can return to what it used to be.

As a Parisian, I'm dismayed to see the positive coverage of the city's policies. Costly, never-ending works make walking (I'm primarily a pedestrian) a nerve-wracking adventure, forcing us to navigate narrow corridors while so-called embellishments go on and on. Cyclists and e-scooter riders whizz down the sidewalks and ignore traffic rules. Meanwhile, stores and eateries are shuttering everywhere. Take a walk down the Avenue Général Leclerc (the General drove down it to liberate Paris in August 1944) and the famous Boulevard Saint-Michel, and lose count of the number of places that have shut down since I came to live in the area in 2005. Excuses are made for this: the growth of suburban shopping malls, COVID, among others.
But the City makes only the feeblest efforts to fine scofflaws and revive business, preferring instead to boast of ""vegetalization"" (now dying in the successive heatwaves) and the reductions in car traffic, while failing to address the knock-on effects of emptier roads.

Launches of any number of new sets ofÂ Magic the Gathering cards go pretty badly, with products routinely snapped up before most can get the site to load, and in-store stock chances few and far between. So it takes a pretty special scale of dreadful for it to result in an unfetteredÂ mea culpa from Wizards of the Coast. That’s what’s followed yesterday’s attempted launch of Chaos Vault’s The Zeta Set, an especially interesting and uniquely designed set of Secret Lair reprints that had the community buzzing. “It was a disaster,” says WotC today. “There’s no other way to say it.”
The Zeta Set was co-created with New York arty bunch MSCHF, designed in part to look like early prototype designs for cards, depicted with collages of stock images and fanzine-like cut-out text, presented in fuzzy, scratchy designs that look incredible. The stand-out card was the new print of Eternity Vessel, here depicted as a photograph of a graded slab of the original card, scored only a 6. Clearly this set was going to be sought after, and so Wizards prepared potential customers with 24 hours of warning ahead of the launch, instructions for a pre-queuing system, and promises of a randomized order of access to purchases for those who showed up an hour early.
Instead what happened, according to those who attempted to buy the cards, was the hour-long queue ahead of launch was paused after just half an hour, then only unpaused 90 minutes later, and eight minutes on from that all stock was gone. Others in the EU report that the set went from “available” to “out of stock” while the queue was frozen. However, even for those who were able to clear the queues still seem to have failed to buy cards. According to Wizards’ apology,
There are reports from customers who believed they had made purchases, but never received a confirmation email, and are currently left in the dark.
“We’re incredibly sorry for the experience,” WotC says in its statement, “and we’re trying to get to the bottom of it.Â We are in the process of investigating exactly what went so very wrong and will share more as soon as we’re able to untangle the mess and understand the root causeâbe it an internal systems issue or external tampering.”
The Hasbro-owned company goes on to say that it “may take a little while to fully get our heads around it,” and describes the whole experience as “frustrating and disappointing” for customers.
Whether you made it through the queue or not, we are going to try and make this right. Once we have a better sense of orders that actually got through, failed orders that we can track, and everything in between, we’ll come back and share as much as we can.
We’ve reached out to WotC to ask for more details regarding the situation, and will update should they get back to us.

9,2026-09-03,"OpenAI wants to sell large enterprises on its technology. Now, it's taking its case to law firms.
The ChatGPT maker is planning to launch new integrations with third-party legal software companies, according to people familiar with the matter.
An OpenAI spokesperson declined to comment.
The company has been in talks with multiple legal software providers about allowing OpenAI's chatbot to plug into their tools and data sources, the people said. This way, lawyers could update a file, negotiate contracts, or search a legal database, without leaving ChatGPT. 
Law firms are under pressure from clients to move faster and show they're not billing associates for work that software can do. In-house legal teams are looking for ways to automate more work themselves, so they can send less to outside counsel and shrink their legal bills.
That demand has produced a flood of startups addressing legal work. For years, those companies have built on top of large language models supplied by frontier research labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic.
Now the labs appear to want a closer relationship with the users themselves. Anthropic and Google have both begun packaging their models as products for legal departments and law firms, selling directly to enterprises rather than only supplying the models themselves.
That effort has been rippling through the public markets. Anthropic's February contract-review plugin helped ignite the ""SaaSpocalypse,"" a stock sell-off driven by fears that AI companies could absorb work once handled by stand-alone software.
OpenAI enters the legal market with an unusual advantage. ChatGPT is already the best-known chatbot among the lawyers it hopes to win over. A Law360 survey found ChatGPT was the profession's most-used tool, with 54% of respondents reporting they use it, ahead of Microsoft Copilot, Gemini, Harvey, and Claude.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
But the consumer brand is also a hurdle in a profession where confidentiality and precision are paramount. Lawyers may trust ChatGPT to plan their holidays in St. Barths. Persuading them that it belongs inside a firm's most sensitive workflows is another matter. Chatbots have already landed lawyers in hot water with judges for making up cases and mangling their meanings.
The company is making its legal push three months after hiring Jason Boehmig, an executive who spent more than a decade figuring out the slow, messy business of selling software to lawyers. He had left a Big Law job to start Ironclad, a company that helps corporate legal departments manage contracts.
Lawyers will be watching OpenAI's launch closely. The tech industry, meanwhile, is about to find out how much room the company intends to leave them.

This is why. It has nothing to do with sound quality. Digital makes these shenanigans possible. Digital data is handled by programmable devices. Programmable devices can be turned against you at the press of a button not if but when the manufacturer wants to. You can't look at something to see if it will do what you want it to do. Software-defined functionality means it can do anything and nothing and you will never know before or even after your money has been spent.
That's the difference between tech enthusiasts and technicians. The tech enthusiast says ""Everything in my house is wired to the Internet of Things! I control it all from my smartphone! My smart-house is bluetooth enabled and I can give it voice commands via Alexa! I love the future!"" but the technician says ""The most recent piece of technology I own is a printer from 2004 and I keep a loaded gun ready to shoot it if it ever makes an unexpected noise.""

The vinyl may not be subject to reprogramming after the fact, but the turntable is . . . an electronic device.
Mine isn't. It has an electric motor, belt, and platter. The arm is fully mechanical. The cartridge has magnets and coils of wire. There's nothing electronic until the preamp, which is not in any way programmable. In fact, there's nothing programmable or Internet-connectable in the whole audio chain. Also, every item is more than 20 years old.

End of the world!  They are making a feature an extra cost which the consumer has the freedom to buy or not.
There was a time, in the US, when seat belts were an extra-cost option. Eventually, some Important People decided this was unsafe; now seat belts are required. Then a requirement for shoulder straps was added, then air bags. This reduced the freedom of drivers to customize their cars but presumably improved safety.
Safety for the driver and passengers. Making critical displays like the speedometer harder to see not only decreases safety for the driver and passengers but also for everyone else on the road.

15,2026-09-03,"Chinese and Western aesthetic tastes diverge wildly, with stuff for sale over there that would never fly over here. Still, it's interesting to peek over the fence and see what they're doing.
This odd-to-us piece of furniture is called a ""turtle stool."" 
 
It's essentially a molded seat attached to a low crate made of aluminum extrusions, which holds two storage bins; according to the designer, who goes by Qingshan (literally ""Green Mountain""), the stool is ""so cozy you'll never want to get up.""


 
The design is interesting in that you can guess how it was designed; Qingshan presumably has access to a bunch of industrial odds and ends—he doesn't claim credit for the seat, only for coming up with the extrusion-made crate—so it was undoubtedly repurposed.




The pieces is sold by Qingshan's brand, Soda Box, and arrives disassembled. 
 
As is the Chinese custom these days, Soda Box has no website for me to link to; the way you purchase pieces like this is through third-party Chinese e-tailer sites. 

I think we do have something akin to this in the states: mechanic's/shop stools, or creeper seats. Little padded seats with optional storage, for scooting around at squat-height while working on a car in a garage. I'll give it to you that the Chinese one looks more suited to everyday lifestyle vs the American one-place, one-purpose use.

16,2026-09-03,"Revenue rose 35% to $1.55bn, adjusted earnings hit 62 cents against 45 expected, and full-year guidance went up. Remaining performance obligations came in at $9bn against the $9.37bn analysts modelled. On a GAAP basis Snowflake lost $191.7m.
Snowflake beat expectations on revenue, on profit and on guidance, and its shares rose more than 20% after hours. One number in the release went the other way, and it is the one that looks furthest into the future.
Remaining performance obligations, the contracted revenue Snowflake has booked but not yet recognised, came in at $9.00bn. Brody Ford reported for Bloomberg that analysts had expected $9.37bn.
Revenue for the quarter ending 31 July reached $1.55bn, up 35% year on year, against a consensus of $1.48bn. Product revenue, which is 96% of the total, rose 37% to $1.49bn against an expected $1.42bn.
Adjusted earnings came in at 62 cents a share. Analysts had forecast 45 cents.
One point worth getting right, because at least one outlet reported it the other way round. Product revenue excludes professional services. The release says so, and the arithmetic confirms it: $1,491.9m of product revenue plus $54.9m of services gives the $1,546.8m total.
The company raised full-year product revenue guidance to $6.07bn, or 36% growth, up from the $5.84bn and 31% it guided in May. For the current quarter it expects $1.588bn to $1.593bn, comfortably above the roughly $1.50bn consensus.
Net revenue retention was 126%. Snowflake added 692 net new customers and now counts 828 spending more than $1m a year.
Remaining performance obligations grew 30% year on year, which sounds healthy in isolation. Against what analysts modelled it is a shortfall of roughly $370m.
Snowflake’s own release explains why the figure is awkward to read. It says the measure “is not necessarily indicative of future product revenue growth because it does not account for the timing of customers’ consumption”, and lists renewals, contract length and seasonality among the things that move it.
Most coverage on Wednesday evening led on the beat and the share price. Bloomberg was the outlet that put the shortfall in print.
Snowflake reported non-GAAP operating income of $237.0m, a 15.3% margin, and non-GAAP net income of $235.3m.
On a GAAP basis it lost money. Operating loss was $263.0m and net loss was $191.7m, or 55 cents a share. That is narrower than the $297.9m loss a year earlier, but it is still a loss.
The bridge between the two is mostly one line. Stock-based compensation-related charges came to $456.4m for the quarter, which is larger than the non-GAAP operating income the results are being celebrated for.
Snowflake lists reducing stock compensation as a share of revenue, and achieving GAAP profitability, among its own risk factors.
None of that is unusual for enterprise software, and the loss is shrinking. It is worth stating plainly because the headline figures being quoted are the adjusted ones.
Chief executive Sridhar Ramaswamy told Reuters that AI products accounted for “approximately half of the acceleration that we are seeing”.
The coding assistant CoCo passed 9,100 accounts, adding more than 2,000 in the quarter. CoWork, which answers questions about a company’s own data, reached 5,800 accounts. Snowflake counts these by averaging the last four weeks of the quarter.
Finance chief Brian Robins called it the third consecutive quarter in which product revenue growth accelerated, and said margin expanded at the same time.
Running AI is not free. Non-GAAP product gross margin was 74.7%, down from about 76% a year earlier on the company’s own figures. In May, Snowflake signed a five-year, $6bn agreement with Amazon Web Services for Graviton processors, which Ramaswamy said locks in what it pays for storage and compute.
Databricks is the comparison Snowflake cannot avoid. We reported last month that it closed a $5bn round at a $190bn valuation.
Databricks has since said it passed $7bn in revenue run-rate, growing more than 80% year on year in its own second quarter. That is more than double Snowflake’s 37%, from a smaller base, and Databricks is private and not obliged to publish audited results.
This lands in an earnings run where AI is the explanation for everything. Nvidia reported $96.2bn for its quarter last week, up 106%.
Disclosure is shifting too. We reported on Wednesday that Microsoft will publish an Azure revenue figure each quarter for the first time, while narrowing what counts as Azure.
And the labs themselves are being measured against each other. Anthropic’s revenue passed OpenAI’s for the first time last quarter.
Snowflake has had a harder AI news cycle than this one. In August, a security vendor attributed a Snowflake flaw to AI-written code, a claim GitHub disputed.
The stock closed at $305.84 before the results and was up 39% for the year to that point. CNBC noted that if Thursday’s open holds the after-hours move, it would be the fourth largest one-day jump since the 2020 listing.
The question the guidance raises is whether consumption holds. Snowflake bills for what customers actually use rather than for seats, so revenue follows usage rather than contracts, which cuts both ways.
Snowflake now has more than 14,500 customers, and 829 of them are on the Forbes Global 2000 list. Its next scheduled disclosure is the Form 10-Q for the quarter just ended.

MicrosoftPlayStationSonyTariffsXbox 
An unprecedented regime of tariffs was implemented by President Trump in 2025. Every console maker raised its prices in the months that followed. Now those same companies are getting massive refunds from the government after the Supreme Court struck down the tariffs as unconstitutional, and gamers are suing for a chunk of the money. As Game File reports, this has put companies like Nintendo, Microsoft, and Sony in the awkward position of telling their customers to take a hike.
In legal terms, that sentiment sounds like what Microsoft and Sony’s lawyers wrote in recent filings knocking down claims that they owe their customers refunds. In the PlayStation 5 manufacturer’s case, it was $508 million back from Uncle Sam. That is more than double what Sony paid to acquire Insomniac Games, the studio behind Marvel’s Wolverine.
âPaying fair market price for voluntarily purchased consumer goods is not a legally cognizable injury in fact,â the companies lawyers wrote to Californiaâs Northern District Court in a request to have the class action lawsuit dismissed, per Game File. Sony went on to claim that that if tariffs were the cause of recent PS5 and PlayStation Portal price hikes, they wouldn’t have raised prices again after the tariffs were declared unconstitutional.
“If tariffs were the cause of the price increases, one would expect SIE to lower prices once the tariffs were eliminatedânot raise them again,” its lawyers argued. “Instead, the timeline confirms that the pricing of PlayStation consoles includes a diverse and dynamic set of input costs.â
Microsoft is ceding no ground in its own defense against a similar class action lawsuit, either. âThere is nothing unjust about Plaintiff purchasing an Xbox at an advertised price and getting exactly what he paid forâregardless of whatever theory he devised months later about Microsoftâs cost structure,” its legal representatives argued. Their defense was, essentially, “prove it.”
âPlaintiff provides no specific allegations that would establish any pricing differential attributable to tariffs or suggest any possibility that Microsoft applied then or could recreate now any dollar-for-dollar calculation of tariff-related pricingâany more than it could any other market factor that affects pricing,” the filing read.
Nintendo made similar points in its own attempt to get the class action lawsuit its facing thrown out. And they all might be right on the legal merits. I’m not a lawyer and this is a somewhat unprecedented situation brought on by one of the dumbest and most chaotic executive branches in the history of the country. But the arguments seem unlikely to be as persuasive in the court of public opinion.

19,2026-09-03,"When Jeremy Spiegel was looking for work in 2009, it was clear why finding a job might be difficult: it was the depths of the Great Recession.
But when he lost his director role at a publishing company in 2024, there was no recession to blame. Spiegel, who's in his 40s and lives in Massachusetts, has spent two years looking for work — despite having more than twenty years of experience.
""I've been told so many times a version of 'It's not you, it's us' that I am beginning to wonder if it is, in fact, me,"" Spiegel said.
He said others have struggled to understand how someone with his background could be out of work for so long. Had he offended someone at the companies where he was applying? one family member wondered. Spiegel said he sometimes avoids social events because he fears he'll have to talk about his job search.
More than a million Americans have been unemployed for at least 27 weeks. While the unemployment rate remains low by historical standards, the share of unemployed Americans who have been out of work for that long has risen considerably since early 2023.
This isn't normal. Historically, when such a large proportion of unemployed Americans have been without work for this long, the unemployment rate has also been elevated, and the US economy is either in or coming out of a recession.
Without such a clear explanation to turn to, many long-term job seekers told Business Insider they've searched for answers about what's holding them back — whether AI and government policy or age discrimination and ghost jobs.
Ofer Sharone, a sociologist at UMass Amherst who studies long-term unemployment, said when job seekers don't have a clear external explanation for why they can't find work, they're more likely to wonder whether something is wrong with them. That self-blame can take a toll on their mental health and make it harder to maintain the confidence needed to keep searching.
""A recession is ironic in that it is a time when finding work is hardest, but also, it is when the stigma of being unemployed is lessened,"" he said.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.

Business Insider is speaking with workers who've found themselves at a corporate crossroads — whether due to a layoff, resignation, job search, or shifting workplace expectations.
Share your story by filling out this form.
After Jon McCarty lost his software engineering job in 2022, he spent 18 months struggling to find another one. He eventually pivoted to handyman work and now works as a part-time cashier at Home Depot.
""I started coming to grips with the idea that I will probably never get another software job,"" said McCarty, who's in his early 60s and lives in Virginia.
McCarty said he'd sometimes made it through phone and technical interviews only to be rejected after a video interview. As the rejections piled up, he began to suspect that his age was hurting his chances. It was one way to make sense of why he kept getting rejected.
""Is this just a perception I've created to make myself feel better about not getting a job? I'm not sure,"" he said.
Sharone said that hope is critical for long-term unemployed jobseekers because their search is a marathon and not a sprint. ""When there is high uncertainty about the causes of the difficulties, it is difficult to maintain this level of hope,"" he said.
For people who have already spent months or years searching for work, having an explanation for their difficult job search ultimately matters less than finding a way out of it.
In the months and years ahead, economic uncertainty could fade and give companies more confidence to hire. The hiring pullback that followed the pandemic-era hiring spree could eventually run its course. Or the US could enter a recession — potentially making an already difficult hiring landscape even worse.
Even in today's difficult job market, some long-term job seekers are managing to break through. But leaving the ranks of the unemployed doesn't always mean the job search ended the way they hoped. Some have given up looking for work altogether, while others have taken jobs that pay considerably less than their previous roles.
When Kymberly McClain resigned from her deputy director role at a small nonprofit in 2024, she was confident she'd quickly find another job. But as months passed without an offer, she began questioning what about her might explain the rejection. Was it her race? Her age? Her lack of a college degree? She even wondered whether she'd somehow ended up on a ""do-not-hire list.""
Nearly two years after her job search began, McClain recently landed a full-time customer-service job at a call center. She said she's making roughly half what she earned in her previous role, but after so much rejection, simply getting an offer helped put at least one of her fears to rest.
""I'm just excited that somebody said yes,"" she said. ""I was like, okay, I'm not blackballed from the job market.""

20,2026-09-03,"Slate expects its no-frills electric truck to start hitting US roads in a few short months.
In emails viewed by Business Insider, the startup asked customers who placed a $300 preorder whether they would be interested in an earlier delivery window. A dropdown menu listed ""December 2026"" as an option.
It's the clearest picture yet of when Slate expects to make its first deliveries. It also lines up with the company's previous guidance, which said the EV trucks would hit US roads in ""late 2026,"" without naming a month.
It doesn't mean that all customers who opt for early delivery will get their cars in December. The emails all included a ""delivery window"" estimating when the cars would ship if they don't nab the December slots. Emails reviewed by Business Insider had estimated ranges from ""coming soon, 2027"" to ""July-September, 2027.""
The Jeff Bezos-backed startup has attracted attention with its bare-bones, $24,950 electric pickup truck. Its base model is gray, has an estimated 205-mile range, gets two seats and crank windows, and comes without a touchscreen or stereo. From there, customers can add features they want, including everything from exterior color wraps and Bluetooth speaker holders to an SUV kit with a second row.
The entire pitch puts Slate in a unique position in the US auto industry. Car makers have increasingly chased high-budget shoppers, helping push the average transaction price for a new car to $49,855 in July, according to Cox Automotive. Small trucks, especially two-door models, are nearly extinct in the US market, too.
Slate says over 180,000 people paid a refundable $50 deposit to hold their spot in a delivery queue. The company opened $300 nonrefundable preorders in June, and said it received over 10,000 deposits in the first few hours after the price was revealed.
Slate's forthcoming rival, the $28,350, all-electric Ford Fathom pickup, will be a big test. For a few thousand more dollars, Ford's new truck will have a native Apple Maps navigation system, four doors, and, likely, power windows. Fathom is expected to reach customers in Fall 2027.

22,2026-09-03,"Look closely at the Nvidia-Hugging Face deal number.
Hugging Face cofounder Thomas Wolf snuck a parenthetical into his announcement of the company's acquisition: ""(special congrats if you find the Hugging Face and Nvidia references hidden in our $12,930,300,000 acquisition price).""
Techies raced to decode the Easter egg.
The Hugging Face reference was easy. The number 129,303 is the decimal representation of Unicode code point U+1F917 — the 🤗 emoji, officially named ""Hugging Face.""
valid! its an emoji of hugging face! pic.twitter.com/PZNGZWjNrE
One commenter pointed out this emoji connection. Wolf responded: ""And there is a second meaning related to Nvidia.""
The Nvidia reference was trickier. Some commenters spotted 93 in the combination, guessing that it was a reference to Nvidia's founding year: 1993.
Some went further, breaking it out into three sets of numbers. 12, 93, and 03. The 12, they said, referenced Nvidia's $12 IPO price. Nvidia went public in 1999 at a price of $12 per share. The stock has skyrocketed, and now trades well over $200.
As for the 03, commenters pointed to Nvidia's three founders: Jensen Huang, Chris Malachowsky, and Curtis Priem.
This should be the solution no?129303 = 🤗 emoji unicode  12 / 93 / 03 = NVIDIA’s $12 IPO / 1993 founding / 3 founders  129303 is basically Hugging Face's hidden easter egg number
For all the number-splitting and decoding, it seems like those commenters got it wrong. Hugging Face CEO Clément Delangue responded to one commenter with a link to the Official Register of Color Names.

The color code 129303, it turns out, is that vibrant shade of green that looks a whole lot like Nvidia's classic color.

24,2026-09-03,"Last year, a federal judge said Google was illegally monopolizing some ad markets.
This week, we learned about the punishment Google is likely to receive for its sins: Not much at all.
While the details of Judge Leonie M. Brinkema’s order remain under seal for now, we have the broad strokes: It will likely have to play nicer with competitors — but in a way that’s unlikely to have any real impact on its business, and certainly not on its core business. Most crucially, it will not have to engage in any structural changes, like splitting itself apart.
“This is pretty close to the best case scenario for them,” ad tech veteran and author Ari Paparo tells me.
If that sounds familiar, there’s a good reason: In 2024, a different federal judge also ruled that Google ran an illegal monopoly in search. The next year, he announced the company’s punishment — which also barely affected Google’s business, and did not require “structural” changes, like selling off parts of itself.
It’s become a recurring theme for Google and its Big Tech peers: The US Government — starting with the first Trump administration, and even more so in the Biden years — has been taking them to court, in an effort to reduce the reach and clout they’ve accumulated over the last couple decades. And the feds keep losing.
In Google’s case, the government has “won” twice, but its prizes are barely consolation prizes.
In other cases, it has been a straight-up loss: Last year, Meta beat the federal suit accusing it of being a monopoly, which means it didn’t have to sell off Instagram or WhatsApp. And in 2022, the US tried to stop Microsoft from buying gaming giant Activision — and lost that case three years later.
And given that the US government has never made a serious attempt to pass legislation reining in Big Tech — except for 2024’s TikTok sell-or-ban law, which everyone basically decided to ignore — court cases were supposed to be the one tool the feds had left. They haven’t worked.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
The federal effort to slow down Big Tech in the courts isn’t formally over. The government is still appealing its Meta loss. And it still has pending cases against Amazon and Apple.
More important is that there are many other anti-Big Tech court cases.
Meta, for instance, just agreed to pay $18 billion to settle a teen addiction suit brought by several US states. Mark Zuckerberg may reasonably call that settlement a bargain, as my colleague Pranav Dixit argues. But it’s a reminder that Big Tech is still dealing with state suits, civil suits filed by users, and a whole lot of regulatory scrutiny in Europe.
All of which means Big Tech is likely to write more checks in the coming years. But investors seem fine with that — Meta’s stock went up after it announced its potential $18 billion payout; Google stock rose after Wednesday's news.
And that reaction makes sense. These fights are over Big Tech as it used to be — this week’s Google news is about a shrinking part of its ad business that may only account for 1% of revenue today — and not where it wants to go, since none of the suits are about the industry’s headlong plunge into AI.
So maybe Washington and others will be able to make Big Tech pay for the old internet. There’s no sign they will keep the companies from owning the next one.

25,2026-09-03,"Governor Mikie Sherrill has signed a law barring municipalities and landlords from blocking portable solar systems of up to 1,200W, a right German tenants and condominium owners have held since October 2024 and which has produced 1.31 million registered systems
Solar panels
New Jersey has legalised plug-in balcony solar up to 1,200W, barring local governments from requiring approvals and stopping landlords from blocking installation. Germany wrote the same right into its civil code in October 2024 and now has 1.31 million registered systems at 1.34GW.
New Jersey has legalised plug-in balcony solar. A new law bars municipalities and landlords from blocking portable panels that plug into a household socket, InsideEVs reported.
Governor Mikie Sherrill signed it this week. It takes effect in six months and caps systems at 1,200W. The panels run through an inverter that stops feeding the grid during an outage, which keeps line workers safe.
Local governments can no longer require licensing or approvals, the governor’s office said. Landlords must be notified 14 days ahead and may still restrict placement and size.
The pitch is bills. Sherrill said the units cost a fraction of rooftop solar and can shave up to $50 off a typical monthly bill. Electricity prices have been climbing across the state.
It passed unanimously in both chambers in June. Around 40% of New Jerseyans rent or live in apartments, a group that has never had a way to lower its own power bill, according to advocacy group Permit Power.
Germany settled this two years ago, after its market had already started without one.
Tenants and condominium owners have had a legal right to install since October 2024, written into the civil code and the condominium act. A landlord must give documented reasons to refuse.
German landlords also cannot demand professional installation. New Jersey’s keep control of placement and size, and both regimes require written notice.
The scale is the difference. Germany had 1.31 million systems registered in April at 1.34GW, and the register is thought to capture a minority of what is actually installed.
The power limits differ too. Germany caps inverter output at 800W against 2,000W of modules, and between 85% and 89% of new systems run at the 800W ceiling. The module allowance is more than twice the feed-in cap.
New Jersey’s 1,200W limit is half as much again. Berlin companies have been building solar for renters on the tighter number for years.
The German market being copied is already cooling. Installations fell 14% year on year in the first quarter, to 64,633 from 88,948.
Britain got there a week earlier anyway. Plug-in kits became legal on 27 August, at around £400 for £70 to £110 of annual saving.

We really, really need to talk about venture capital. Because it’s not “venture capital” anymore.
There’s a huge disconnect between what most people think of VC, where an investor has a big fund and cuts checks to help a founder build a company, and the current reality, where a handful of billionaire extremists use the cover of “VC” to advance an outrageous agenda where they’re accountable to no one.
I’m gonna explain this from a standpoint that almost never gets articulated: I’ve personally raised tens of millions of dollars in venture capital funding as CEO of startups, and been directly involved as a board member or advisor in raising hundreds of millions more. I’ve sat in board rooms, across the table from the people I’m talking about here, or been at the industry events that they frequent. So this isn’t sour grapes because these VCs wouldn’t cut me a check, or some chip on my shoulder about these investors due to a business deal. This is what I know about these bad actors because I’m part of the community of creators and inventors who build the things that they used to invest in — back when they still cared about innovation.
Many of the trends in society and politics that people are most angry about, from data centers being forced down everyone’s throats, to all of our favorite apps and services being enshittified, to politicians being paid to ignore the will of the people, are all being supercharged by these cancer capitalists. They have warped the structure of venture capital into a form of oligarchy that answers to no market, no regulators, and no voters. So it’s worth understanding exactly how they did it.
I’ll be breaking these points down in further detail, but just to begin framing the concept, I’ll lay out the core idea here in some bullet points (so that you’re not tempted to run this whole thing through an LLM):
All of this self-dealing, and the way that they’re isolated from any accountability, has made these firms become more and more shameless in their behavior. Former Andreessen Horowitz partner John O’Farrell publicly called out the firm (a rarity — the company is notoriously vindictive towards those who it decides are disloyal) for what he called its “political infiltration” of AI policy. Marc Andreessen, Ben Horowitz and their firm have put $115.3 million into this midterm cycle — nearly double their $63 million in 2024, and more than any other billionaire donor in the country, even including Elon Musk. Molly White, whose Tech Influence Watch tracks this money against FEC filings, shows that a16z alone accounts for more than 20% of all political contributions from the entire cohort of crypto and AI companies it follows. And they’re funneling these funds to candidates in both parties. This is a huge escalation from the tentative baby steps that folks like Zuckerberg were making in the Obama era, working on benign issues like trying to help immigrants.
And of course, it gets a lot worse than just their lobbying. As I have frequently noted, Andreessen Horowitz hired a man as a partner at their firm despite his having no background or qualifications in tech, finance, or startups whatsoever. His only discernible qualification was that he had choked my unarmed neighbor Jordan Neely to death on a subway car.
This is how brazen, how toxic and destructive, we’ve allowed the industry formerly known as venture capital to become. We must understand that it is no longer a financial machine that is used to fund startups, but a political and social machine focused on dismantling democracy and civil society. And it’s time to act accordingly.
Up next: we’ll dive into the specifics of many of the points laid out above, to understand more about how we got here.
