28,2026-09-03,"55+ senior communities and similar age-restricted living communities present a unique problem to young would-be homeowners. As does the social dynamic surrounding them. All of this is pushed to a head by the modern state of the housing market. 
There has been a story circulating over the last couple of weeks about a 28-year-old woman who is being forced out of her home by her HOA. The home is in a senior living community, which requires occupied homes to meet its age restrictions. The HOA board and some motivated residents have been aiming to force the woman out of the home in accordance with those age-restriction rules.
To summarize the various different stories on this..
 
HOA Votes Young Woman Out of 55+ Seniors Community For Being 'Too Young' After Inheriting a House
Story sources: Inside Edition via reddit
Bethany, a 28-year-old woman moved into her military veteran father's home in a 55+ community to care for him. 
After he passed away in 2023, she inherited the home. Which she says he specifically wanted her to have. The problem was that the HOA rules require occupied homes to meet its age restrictions. So, the HOA told the woman that she could no longer live there because she was too young. 
The HOA held a board vote and voted 5-to-0 to force her out.
But she refused to leave.
 
What began as an inheritance and residency dispute escalated into legal battles. Now the HOA revoked her access to community amenities and proposed a $155,000 special assessment to evict her that would require roughly 155 homeowners to help fund the HOA's legal efforts. 
 
The controversy has divided residents, particularly because some say they support the woman remaining in the home and object to spending thousands of dollars to remove a neighbor who has caused them no problems.
 
Rules are rules but are they still relavent in this day and age? 
""I have a right, and every right to stay in my home,"" Bethany, the 28-year-old woman, was quoted as telling Inside Edition.
But some residents are insistant that she be removed.
""It's time for her to leave…""
""It's time for her to leave…"" one resident, a 74-year-old, was quoted as saying, while her own adult daughter added by wondering why the 28-year-old woman would want to live there.
Meanwhile, angry residents at the board meeting could be seen calling her out for her age and stating that they didn't move to the community to be around young people. This culminated in the 5-0 vote to force her out.
---
Really, I imagine this whole meeting going down like the ending of a Survivor episode…
""Bethany, you have been voted out of the retirement community. It's time to bring us your torch.""
Bethany, the tribe has spoken. 
I see it all like this…
To a millennial who hasn't been able to purchase a home themselves, inheriting one that is affordable and easy to maintain on a tight budget is like winning the lottery. And I can see why she would be reluctant to just give that up. Really, she should just get a 55-year-old roommate and circumvent the HOA's rule entirely.
But this does highlight some other glaring issues with senior-only living communities when young people are no longer able to enter the property ladder.
I think this story highlights a more concerning trend in real estate ownership and the different stages that owning a home used to take throughout our lives. A trend that has been flipped on its head thanks to, well, everything else that has changed socially and culturally, and of course financially, between generations.
When our grandparents graduated high school, in their late teens or early 20s, they bought a 4- to 6-bedroom home. For themselves and for their growing family of 3 to 4 or more children. This, all supported on one salary of a high-school-educated working spouse.
When our parents graduated from university, in their still early or mid to late 20s, they bought a 2-bedroom house for their 1-2 children. With one spouse quitting their career to care for the kids full time. Then, with equity accumulated from that first starter home, they upgraded into a larger family home some time later.
Now, today, take a couple. Two college-educated millennials, on two well-above-average incomes, well into their 30s. No kids. Maybe a dog or a cat. They are only just now considering whether they might be able to afford that 1-to 2-bedroom starter home. That home that their parents bought in their 20s.
Except it's starting to get a bit late in life to start a family. If they want to have a family, a 1-or-2-bedroom home for the next 10 years isn't going to cut it. Maybe, they should just skip the starter home altogether. Stretch the budget into a 3-or-4-bedroom home off the bat. Accepting, with a grimace, that it's going to take another 10 years to pay off the mortgage due to the higher loan and snowballing interest. But that's just the reality of the situation.
Except, when they start looking at properties, they quickly meet the other reality of the situation. It's not just other couples or prospective families that they're competing with for real estate.
Now, historically, up until now, real estate has followed a cycle. A circle of life that follows our own: You buy the smaller first home in your early 20s. You upsize into a larger one when you have more equity and need the space for a growing family. You then might upsize or move again when your family grows beyond that or move as your career(s) progress.
Then, the kids move out. Then you retire. Now, you're living on a pension, tighter finances, and a finite supply of money that needs to last you the rest of your life. So you start to look at your large home and wonder if you really need all that space. The maintenance time and cost required present a significant, growing burden. And much of the house is sitting empty even when family comes to visit. So, you sell that big house to the next family, coming into that stage of life, who needs it. And you settle for a smaller place, 2 maybe 3 bedrooms is fine, just as little outside maintenance required as possible, please.
Except this cycle has been defied. Cashed-up Boomers and Gen-Xers don't need to worry about downsizing.
They have ridden the wave of a property market and economy that offered strong wages and investment opportunities. An environment that passively grew their income to a point that their wealth dwarfs that of any previous generations at their age.  And eclipses the buying power and earning potential of any younger generations today.  
So downsizing is out of the question. It's viewed as a moral and social embarrassment among their peers. Heck, they might even look at upsizing again or look to buy an investment property to grow their wealth even further. They're still just trying to accrue capital like their life depends on it, even though they have more than enough to live off of.
What do they need the extra money for? Who knows, but maybe they're probably planning to bury their cash and investment portfolio with them like a Pharaoh to take it into the afterlife.
So, the entire cycle of buying and selling property. That circle of life itself has been upended.
Retirees and seniors mingle amid families at open homes. Maybe they're looking to move closer to family, but they're still refusing to consider downsizing. So the price of family homes continues to climb as those same seniors outmuscle families for real estate. 
Stressed-out first home buyers and looking-to-upsize but stretched-thin growing families look on in horror as they are out-offered again. Yet another prospective property slips out of their grasp.
Meanwhile, 55+ communities, 60+ units, and other senior living arrangements now languish on the market. Their intended buyers are instead collecting a portfolio of family homes. Affordable real estate within senior communities sits empty, young people would happily move in, but they are barred from even entering the contest there thanks to HOA rules.
See, I think that is the real problem on display here.
Why are there senior living communities where those under 55 are banned from ownership? Yet there are no family living communities where seniors are similarly banned from ownership?
Maybe we should have HOAs and real estate communities where those who are 55+ are barred from owning real estate. That might actually help to set the cycle, the circle of life, right again.
In fact, let's make this the case for all family-sized or starter homes, or any home within a school district that might be desirable to a growing family.
It is, after all, only fair.
 
As usual, see some of the responses from an online thread where this story was shared, below.

Tech > Computing > Gadgets > Robots
The RockAqua P1 made its splashdown at IFA 2026 in Berlin, and we saw it first.
Managing Editor / Home and Kitchen
Managing Editor / Home and Kitchen
September 3, 2026, 7:41 am ET
Roborock, one of the flashiest home robot makers, is taking the plunge. The Beijing-based brand, known for its super-smart robot vacuums, including models with robotic grabbing arms and stair-climbing capabilities, has launched its first-ever robotic pool cleaner. 
We saw it first at IFA Berlin.
On a crowded showroom floor, we got a look at Roborock’s small and colorful new pool cleaner. Dubbed the RockAqua P1, this autonomous underwater vacuum is engineered to clean pool floors, walls, waterlines and shallow platforms. 
Its adaptive cleaning path, a feature prominent in Roborock’s indoor vacuums, allows the robot to navigate pools of different shapes and depths. Vision-based obstacle avoidance identifies obstacles such as drain covers and steps, helping reduce interruptions during cleaning.
While obstacle avoidance is useful, the ability to track down debris in a pool using AI cognition, as our top-rated Aiper Scuba V3 does, is even more impressive. So far, it doesn’t look like Roborock’s entry into the pool-cleaner category offers a comparable debris-detection feature.
What the RockAqua P1 lacks in proactive leaf, bug, and stone hunting, it makes up for with a lot of suction power. The robot delivers a suction rate of 6,800 gallons per hour, placing it in the same class as some of the most powerful pool-cleaning robots from well-established brands. Still, our lab testing shows that raw power doesn’t necessarily translate to better cleaning.
One particularly interesting feature is the robot’s ability to adjust its suction depending on the situation. According to Roborock, when it encounters larger debris, the robot reduces suction to prevent the water flow from disturbing the debris and pushing it out of the cleaning path. As it gets closer, it increases suction to capture the debris.
Roborock’s first pool cleaner runs for up to 3.5 hours on a full charge, which is average for high-end models. When a cleaning cycle is complete, the robot rests on the water’s surface. That’s a useful touch, since some pool cleaners remain at the bottom and have to be hauled out manually. The surface-resting design should make the RockAqua P1 easier to retrieve for emptying and recharging.
As of now, the RockAqua P1 is set for release in the European market only, with a rollout to the US still in question.

Tech > Mobile > Phones
Apple's memory costs for some of the new phones could be nearly 400% higher than last year, TrendForce says.
Senior Consumer Insights Editor
Senior Consumer Insights Editor
September 3, 2026, 5:09 pm ET
Apple’s “Surprise and Shine” event is days away, and there are plenty of rumors swirling about new devices we can expect, like a new Apple Watch and the first foldable iPhone, which could be called the iPhone Ultra. 
When we asked CNET readers one thing they’d change about the iPhone in our Big Guessing Game, 16% said they want it to cost less. But that’s not happening. In fact, you should expect new models to cost significantly more.  
TrendForce, a global market research firm, released a report on Thursday suggesting you can expect higher prices instead of steep discounts. The firm expects the iPhone 18 series to cost 10 to 20% more due to the ongoing RAM shortage and higher hardware costs. 
“For the 256GB Pro model, memory costs in [the third quarter of 2026] are expected to be nearly 400% higher than a year earlier,” TrendForce said. “Despite Apple’s efforts to negotiate lower prices for other components, these savings are unlikely to offset the resulting pressure on overall [materials] costs.”
Apple raised prices on select products in June due to the RAM shortage, but excluded iPhones from the price hikes. Now there’s a chance that Apple could release some iPhone 18 models with higher prices at the September event. TrendForce said it expects Apple to absorb some of the costs to keep prices for the new models as part of a “relatively moderate” pricing strategy. 
So exactly how much more are we talking about? Some analysts believe the foldable iPhone Ultra could cost between $2,000 and $2,500. TrendForce predicts the starting price will be slightly higher — between $2,099 and $2,299. The phone could go up to $3,000. TrendForce didn’t immediately respond to a request for further comment. 
But higher costs aren’t equivalent to big improvements. 
TrendForce says we shouldn’t expect major performance upgrades from Apple’s upcoming iPhone lineup. Instead, we should expect a more powerful processor for AI features and power efficiency, better chip cooling technology and power-efficient displays. And the iPhone 18 Pro models’ main camera could have a “mechanical variable aperture” to improve depth-of-field control and dynamic range for different lighting settings. 
The really big upgrade is the potential release of the folding iPhone Ultra and bigger batteries for premium iPhone 18 models, TrendForce predicts. The iPhone 18 Pro Max could see the biggest upgrade due to a larger chassis. CNET’s Patrick Holland tested smartphone batteries last year and found that the iPhone 17 Pro Max was the best for battery life, so iPhone 18’s premium models could be an even bigger upgrade from the current model.

These combo models can heat, cool and clean air all in the same unit.
Smart Home & Robotics Editor
Smart Home & Robotics Editor
September 3, 2026, 11:03 am ET
Dyson’s HushJet air purifiers have been some of the versatile air purifiers on the market, able to cool, purify and heat the air, so you get climate control capabilities in addition to purification. The new HushJet purifier range, announced at the IFA 2026 trade show in Berlin, incorporates all of these capabilities with a complete redesign and I was able to see all three models in person prior to the show. The new HushJet models look like large curved pipes, a design Dyson calls the “HushJet Nozzle.” It incorporates the directed-fan capabilities of the HushJet MiniCool Fan that we loved for portable summer cooling, with the aerospace turbine design of the HushJet Compact, for more consistent performance and reduced noise and turbulence. The largest and most impressive of the units is the $1,099 Dyson HushJet Big+Quiet Cool Pure+Formaldehyde. The name is a mouthful, but it’s the company’s largest air purifier, with the most powerful compressor, designed to handle multiroom air purification and mixing. It even has wheels so it can be moved from room to room (it’s not self-propelled, unfortunately), and controls that let the nozzle-like head move and rotate in a way that’s vaguely reminiscent of a Dune sandworm. The head can tilt up and down, letting you angle the airflow for direct cooling or broader room circulation. 
Impressively, despite the unit’s sheer size, I didn’t really hear the airflow except at the highest settings. Meanwhile, the sheer amount of air it emitted through the large, open demo area was impressive. I could really feel the air circulating and starting to clear up without it feeling like I was being blown directly by a fan. The unit can capture 99.99% of pollutants down to 0.01 microns and destroy formaldehyde, a chemical byproduct that can be present in many homes. 
The medium-sized $599 HushJet Pure Cool+Formaldehyde is the unit I expect most people to get because it’s on the more reasonable side while still delivering powerful purification and cooling. Dyson says it can deliver three times the purification of predecessors and still clean large spaces while acting as a fan to provide personal cooling. 
The $599 HushJet Hot Cool Pure+ is similar, but it adds heating capabilities in addition to the purification and cooling. Dyson describes it as a three-in-one unit that can also be useful as a space heater in the winter, not just a fan in the summer.All three Dyson HushJet models are expected to launch in fall 2026, and we look forward to testing them at CNET Labs.

33,2026-09-03,"American shoppers are waving some red flags that hint at a recession in the next year, one legendary economist is warning.
Gary Shilling, the Stanford economist and Merrill Lynch alum best-known for calling the dot-com bubble and 2008 housing crisis, is doubling down on his view that the US will likely enter a recession sometime in the coming 12 months.
In a note this week, Shilling pointed to one particualr area of concern in the economy: the US consumer.
Households are flashing warning signs as energy costs remain elevated and interest rates look poised to remain higher for longer.
""Consumers are signaling that all is not great with the economy,"" Shilling wrote. ""We continue to think there's a 60% to 70% chance that a recession unfolds in the next year. We also don't rule out a period of stagflation with high inflation, elevated employment and slow economic growth,"" he later added.
Stagflation, a worst-case scenario for the economy where growth slows while inflation remains high, is thought to be even harder for policymakers to deal with than a typical recession, as high inflation prevents the Fed from lowering rates to boost the economy as it would do in a typical recession.
Consumer spending is a major growth engine in the US economy, accounting for around two-thirds of GDP. The upper tier of earners in the US continues to spend, but the fear has been that lower- and middle-income households are pulling back as they feel the pressure of a higher cost of living on multiple fronts. 
Here are the consumer warning signs that Shilling sees:
Revised retail sales data showed that Americans pulled back significantly on spending in July. Retail sales declined 0.6% for the month — the worst monthly decline recorded in nearly a year, according to Census data.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
""A big factor in July's weak numbers was the fading of federal tax refund money, which in recent months has likely been spent not so much on discretionary purchases as on more-expensive, and non-discretionary, gasoline and groceries,"" Shilling said.
Consumers are pessimistic about the state of the economy. The University of Michigan's consumer sentiment gauge dropped for the first time in three months in August, with the index down sharply from its peak in early 2020.
Some of the pessimism has to do with higher inflation expectations. Year-ahead inflation expectations clocked in at around 4% in August compared to 3.4% in February, before the start of the Iran war, per UMich's survey.
71% of middle-income Americans said they believed they were falling behind the cost of living, according to a second-quarter Primerica survey. 59% said they believed the economy would be worse off over the next year.
Consumer credit liabilities among households and nonprofit organizations grew to a record $5.1 trillion at the end of the first quarter, according to Fed data.
32% of middle-income Americans said their credit card debt grew over the last quarter, according to the Primerica survey.
Bankruptcy filings are also rising. The number of Americans filing for bankruptcy rose 12% in the 12 months leading up to the end of June, Shilling said.
The personal savings rate, a reflection of how cash-strapped Americans are, has dropped significantly over the last five years. Americans socked away an average 3% of their disposable personal income in July, down from a personal savings rate of over 9% in July of 2021, according to BEA data.
""Strapped consumers, digging themselves deeper into debt, are draining their piggybanks,"" Shilling wrote.
Some mega-retailers are struggling. Shilling pointed in particular to Walmart, which has seen its stock price crater 20% from its peak in May of this year.
The latest decline for the stock was triggered by its second-quarter earnings report, which showed signs that more shoppers are pulling back. Comparable sales, a measure of current revenue that accounts for factors like new store openings, grew at its slowest pace in six years. Average spending per transaction also slowed sharply to 1.1%, down from 3.1% a year ago.
Dollar stores and other discount retailers, on the other hand, are thriving. Comparable sales rose 3.7% at Dollar Tree, 3.5% at Dollar General, and 14.1% at Five Below over the last quarter.
""The move to these types of outlets has been going on for several years as inflation spiked, first in 2021 and 2022 in the aftermath of the pandemic and then again starting last year amidst Trump's tariffs and the effects of the ongoing war in Iran,"" Shilling added.

34,2026-09-03,"Banks ordered more than $30bn for a facility that started at $20bn, and the margin fell to 68 basis points over SOFR from 85 on the last deal
Singapore, 2024: Close up ByteDance company logo on office building. 
ByteDance has landed a $29.6bn loan, the second-largest in Asia this year and roughly three times what the TikTok owner borrowed offshore in 2024. Banks put in more than $30bn of orders for a facility that started life at $20bn.
The terms were first reported by Bloomberg. Citigroup and JPMorgan are coordinating the deal, which runs for three years with an option to extend to five, and it has not yet been signed while banks confirm allocations.
The pricing is the part lenders will be arguing about. ByteDance is paying an opening margin of 68 basis points over SOFR, down from the 85 basis points it paid on its last offshore facility, which means it borrowed half again as much money at a materially lower price.
That happened in a year when Asia’s loan market has otherwise been slow. Demand for this particular borrower has moved in the opposite direction to the market around it.
The trajectory is steep. ByteDance raised $10.8bn from around 20 lenders in 2024, and was in preliminary talks over a record $20bn facility as recently as June, a target the final deal has now overshot by nearly half.
That 2024 facility was, at the time, the largest dollar corporate loan in Asia outside Japan. Two years on, the same borrower has taken almost three times as much and still finished second in the region, which says as much about the year as it does about ByteDance.
The stated use is general corporate purposes, which in ByteDance’s case currently means data centres. The company is weighing capital spending of up to $70bn a year on AI infrastructure, a figure that would put it in the same bracket as the American hyperscalers.
It is also spending under constraint. US export controls limit its access to Nvidia’s advanced chips, so the build-out runs through custom silicon on Arm and RISC-V, Qualcomm inference parts, and domestic Chinese suppliers, which makes the same amount of compute more expensive to assemble.
Some of that budget goes to a competitor. ByteDance has been paying more than $1bn a year to use OpenAI’s models through Microsoft Azure while financing the domestic hardware base intended to end that dependence.
Only one Asian borrower has raised more this year. SoftBank signed a $40bn bridge facility in March to fund its OpenAI position, and the two deals now bookend the region’s AI borrowing.
The structures are not comparable, though. SoftBank’s facility is a bridge against an equity stake, repaid when longer-term financing arrives, while ByteDance is a cash-generating business borrowing against its own operations.
That distinction is what the 68 basis points is pricing. Lenders are treating TikTok and Douyin revenue as the collateral, rather than a mark on a private company that could move.
The wider pattern is now well established, with Big Tech’s AI-related debt passing $350bn as companies fund data centres with borrowed money rather than cash flow. ByteDance joining at this size moves a large share of that borrowing outside the US.
What the money buys is a run at the frontier. ByteDance is training a ten-trillion-parameter model and expanding its data centre cluster in Inner Mongolia, neither of which is affordable on operating cash flow alone.
There is an unresolved figure in the reporting. The $70bn capex number sits awkwardly against the 160 billion yuan, around $22.7bn, that ByteDance was reported to be planning for 2026 earlier in the year, and the sources differ on whether $70bn is a decision or a scenario under consideration.
The company has not commented publicly on either number. It remains privately held, publishes no financial statements, and discloses capital spending only through the people who arrange its financing.
Which is the quiet significance of a syndicated loan this size. Twenty-odd banks now have a view of ByteDance’s books that its users, its advertisers and most of its regulators do not.

36,2026-09-03,"Apple may introduce an entirely new 13.8-inch OLED MacBook model in 2029, potentially creating a new product tier between the MacBook Air and MacBook Pro, according to an updated product roadmap from research firm Omdia.
The firm's latest roadmap sandwiches a new 13.8-inch MacBook between the projected time line for the Air and Pro models. The surprise model is said to use a single-stack hybrid OLED panel, rather than the more sophisticated tandem OLED technology that is expected in the upcoming OLED MacBook Pro models, which are set to launch either later this year or early next year.
It seems this prediction hasn't come out of nowhere, either. According to a June report from ZDNet Korea, Apple sounded out Samsung Display, LG Display, and BOE for information regarding a 13.8-inch OLED MacBook panel earlier this year. At the time, the general assumption was that it was likely to be part of Apple's work on the first OLED MacBook Air, expected by 2028 at the earliest.
However, looking at Omdia's latest roadmap, the OLED MacBook Air is conspicuously absent, having been replaced by the 13.8-inch OLED machine in a separate ""new"" product category. The new model is expected to incorporate an LTPO+ backplane, integrated touch sensing, an under-panel camera, and a color filter integrated into the OLED encapsulation layer. 
Omdia believes it is still too early to know for sure which display manufacturer would supply panels for the device, but the firm believes that it could potentially become an important mainstream MacBook product, positioned between the LCD MacBook Air and the higher-end OLED MacBook Pro.
Meanwhile, the MacBook Air line is shown as continuing to use LCD displays right into 2030.
Of course, Apple's wholesale OLED adoption plans could change, and Omdia itself caveats that its roadmap is based on current market expectations. On that basis alone, it could be adjusted at any time according to OLED manufacturing capacity, component costs, and Apple's wider product strategy. Additionally, this is the first we've heard of a completely new MacBook line, and it's still three years out. So with all due caution, file this one under ""wait and see.""

Cloud GamingGame PassXboxxCloud 
Right now, cloud streaming with Xbox Game Pass is unlimited. In November, that will no longer be the case. That’s when Microsoft is going to start setting monthly caps on cloud gaming usage, even for the highest-paying members. The company tried to soften the stealth price hike by pointing out that very few people are using cloud gaming that much to begin with. “We expect this upcoming change to impact 4% of Game Pass subscribers,” Microsoft wrote on Thursday.
Starting in November, these will be the new xCloud limits for each tier of Game Pass:
At the same time, Microsoft says it will allow players to begin paying for cloud gaming by the hour, even if they don’t have a Game Pass subscription, though it hasn’t yet disclosed what that price will be. Metering the service makes sense. Why have Game Pass subscribers who never use the feature pay to subsidize those who use it almost exclusively? At the same time, it’s not like this change is coming alongside a lower Game Pass price for those other folks. The service is losing a benefit while the price stays the same.
“Weâre introducing this model because the cost of providing cloud gaming grows as more people use it and play for longer,” Microsoft explained in a blog post. “Moving to monthly limits allows us to keep offering the service while continuing to invest in its reliability and performance. We understand that for some players the practical result is a higher cost. In November, weâll provide more information about pricing, purchasing additional hours, eligible games, market availability, and how players can check their remaining time.”
In the meantime, Microsoft has already begun testing a free, ad-supported tier of cloud gaming.

		Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.	
Tech > Computing
Project AeroBlade is an exciting new and fanless cooling system, while Project Swan builds on a motorized display concept shown off at CES.
Writer
Writer
September 3, 2026, 12:00 pm ET
When it comes time for a big computing show, you can always count on Lenovo to exhibit some wacky proof-of-concept machines.
At CES 2026, for instance, the Chinese company debuted an AI-powered tracking webcam and a horizontal rollable gaming laptop display. Just this year, my personal favorite gaming handheld prototype debuted at Mobile World Congress, alongside a detachable dual-screen ThinkBook concept. Now, Lenovo’s coming out swinging again with its IFA 2026 exhibition – the company debuted two new concepts this time around in Berlin.
Project Swan is a 14-inch consumer laptop that can easily be toted around from place to place, before its horizontally rolling screen extends to 17 inches for work or entertainment. Project AeroBlade introduces a completely new ventless cooling system that minimizes noise pollution and supposedly doesn’t require any major compromises in compute performance.
If theoretical and fantastical concepts aren’t what you’re looking for, you’re boring and probably lack whimsy. But fret not, because Lenovo debuted plenty of products that it’ll be bringing to market over the course of the next 12 months, including a new type of OLED monitor, beefy (and expensive) desktop PCs and a handful of new consumer laptops.
Lenovo has never shied away from funky device displays. The company released or has shown off concepts for computers with vertical motorized rolling screens, laptop displays that wrap around the top lid to show off widgets while closed and screens that turn to keep you centered in the built-in webcam. At this year’s Mobile World Congress, Lenovo even showcased a prototype handheld gaming console with a folding screen that works in horizontal and vertical configurations.
The company is keeping the ball rolling at IFA with Project Swan, its first horizontally expanding, rollable work laptop. The 14-inch device can extend into a 17-inch display at the touch of a button. It’s a very large screen packed into one of the most portable clamshell designs possible.
The Legion Pro Rollable, shown off at CES 2026, uses much the same technology — but that was a gaming-centric laptop, tailored toward first-person shooters and other games that benefit from a wide field of view. I’m not sure how folks will respond to the deluge of laptops with rollable displays, but by my estimation, the Project Swan concept is one of the most practical Lenovo has shown off yet.
This seems like the starting point for a solid workhorse laptop: I can imagine using it for work and packing it away for trips, then unfurling the screen to use the computer for periods of high-productivity multitasking – or as a portable entertainment center for movies and shows. If this concept ever comes to fruition, it might be the most family-oriented rollable in Lenovo’s stable.
I don’t know about you, but I’m tired of singeing my legs when I push my laptop a little too hard and the vents dump all that excess heat right into my lap. I also hate when I’m bundled up under a blanket and the blocked fans start whirring concerningly loud.
Lenovo’s Project AeroBlade feels like the type of concept device that would tangibly improve just about any laptop user’s experience. This 14-inch ultraportable laptop has a new fanless design that Lenovo says won’t compromise performance. Instead, there’s a single vent running along the hinge, which allows outside air to pass through the internal thermals. And moving air through the laptop aren’t fans but tiny modules that use ultrasonic vibrations. Lenovo calls this vibrating cooling tech AirJet.
At a press event in New York, a member of Lenovo’s design team explained that the new cooling design is more akin to “a bike pump than a leaf blower.” Whereas the average laptop dumps heat in a single direction, Project AeroBlade pulls in cool air from its surroundings and circulates it through the inside of the laptop.
Whether or not this iteration of Project AeroBlade comes to fruition, it’ll be interesting to see if Lenovo’s new cooling solution ever makes it to the market. A cooler, quieter and higher-performance system feels like more than a marketing gimmick – if it works as advertised, this could seriously sway consumers toward a better type of laptop design.
If you’re looking for a best-in-class monitor, odds are good you’re looking for a display with an OLED panel – the quicker response time gives the tech an edge over IPS LCDs. But while OLED monitors have become significantly more affordable over the past decade, they’re still a bit too pricey for some folks.
Those prices may start to come down an appreciable amount again in the near future, thanks to new inkjet-printing OLED tech. These displays literally use a printer to deposit organic material on a glass layer thousands of times per second.
The result is a display that doesn’t compromise color contrast or picture quality, though it hasn’t achieved anywhere close to the same level of brightness as traditional OLEDs. Some analysts believe the IJP OLEDs might cut production costs by up to 20%, and some of those savings will be passed on to the consumer.
Lenovo isn’t the first to create a consumer IJP OLED screen, but the ThinkVision P27UL-40 is the first inkjet-printed monitor the company is bringing to the market. This 27-inch monitor has a 3,840×2,160-pixel resolution panel and an up to 120Hz refresh rate.
The ThinkVision P27UL-40 will be released in March 2027, starting at $1,300, which is still a significant chunk of change. But if industry analysts are right, this might be the start of a trend leading to more competitively priced OLED monitors.
Other ThinkVision monitors also debuted at IFA 2026, but none of those models use the same IJP OLED technology as the ThinkVision P27UL-40. Instead, they’re rather standard LCD monitors.
The ThinkVision T32WD-4v is a 32-inch IPS monitor with 3,840×2,160-pixel resolution and an up to 120Hz refresh rate. It’ll start shipping in February next year and start at $900. 
The ThinkVision T34WD-4v is the larger addition to the ThinkVision T series lineup: It’s a 34-inch VA monitor with 3,440×1,440-pixel resolution and an up-to-120Hz refresh rate. That monitor will start shipping in January with a starting price of $1,000.
If you’ve got enough pocket change to ignore the surging hardware costs spurred by the RAMageddon, maybe Lenovo’s new ThinkCentre X Ultra desktop PC is for you. This veritable powerhouse is an “AI-first” juggernaut, with a design crafted to optimize compute power (though a computer like this could realistically handle most tasks you throw at it).
This 4.4-pound computer is powered by AMD silicon, housing an up-to-Ryzen AI Max Plus Pro 495 processor. It features integrated graphics, up to 128GB of RAM and 8TB of system storage. There are two expansion slots for additional SSDs, assuming you have the cash for upgrades after buying this desktop. The ThinkCentre X Ultra will be released in November at a starting price of $3,700.
Alongside the ThinkCentre X Ultra, Lenovo is debuting two other desktop PCs. The 2.6-pound ThinkCentre M75q Gen 6 is even more compact than the Ultra, but still retains some beefy AMD processing power. This desktop is powered by up to Ryzen AI 7 Pro 450GE silicon, has integrated Radeon 860M graphics, up to 64GB of RAM and 4TB of storage space. The M75q Gen 6 will be available later this month at a starting price of $1,380.
The ThinkCentre M75s Gen 6, on the other hand, is the largest desktop PC in the new lineup. Weighing in at 10.6 pounds, this computer is powered by an up-to-Ryzen AI 7 Pro 450G processor, 64GB of RAM and 4TB of storage. Different configurations come with integrated Radeon 860M and Radeon RX 6400 8GB graphics. The M75s Gen 6 will be available next month at a starting price of $1,490.
Lenovo may not have debuted any wacky new ThinkBook concepts at IFA, but the company showed off two new 14-inch iterations of its popular work laptop lineup that will begin shipping soon.
The ThinkBook 14 Gen 9 is a balanced 2.8-pound laptop, packaging a decent 14-inch, 1,920×1,200-pixel resolution display and solid internal specs into a single clamshell. It’s powered by a Qualcomm Snapdragon X2 Plus processor and a Qualcomm Adreno GPU, and has up to 32GB of LPDDR5x RAM and a 1TB SSD (with the option to slot in a second SSD). The ThinkBook 14 Gen 9 will be released next month with a starting price of $1,300.
The ThinkBook 14x Gen 2 is the sleek ultraportable offering, weighing just under 2.2 pounds. Lenovo packed some decent hardware into this clamshell despite its diminutive size, and the optional 2.8K OLED screen will provide snappy color contrast that’ll satisfy most folks.
The ThinkBook 14x Gen 2 is powered by Intel Core 7 Series 3 silicon, integrated graphics, up to 32GB of LPDDR5x memory and 2TB of storage space. It will be released in early 2027 and start at $1,000.

HTC may not be in the phone business any longer, but the company has discovered new niches to fill, as we’ve seen with the HTC Vive VR platform. This time last year, we heard about plans to expand Vive into smart glasses with the launch of the Vive Eagle smart glasses. Earlier this summer, we learned that the Vive Eagle was finally coming to the US in September, and today we’ve got the full details on that debut, ready to share.
The Vive Eagle take a glasses-first approach to smart eyewear, and that means a priority on a sleek, comfortable fit — and that also means one not encumbered by screens. That makes these feel like a direct competition to the likes of Meta’s smart glasses.
For the US market, HTC is giving us a choice of two frame styles in a variety of colors: “square” frames (above) in coffee, black, and berry red, as well as round frames (below) in charcoal, clear brown, and navy. You also have a pick of lens options — there’s a Zeiss lens by default, but you can also go with sunglasses or even photochromic lenses.
The hardware is equipped with 4GB of RAM and is powered by Qualcomm’s Snapdragon AR1 chip. The onboard 12MP ultrawide camera records up to 10 minutes of video at once, and can even do slo-mo for a minute straight. You can livestream to YouTube or Twitch, and capture in 2K resolution vertical format for social media. You’ve got 32GB of encrypted on-board storage to house your clips.
Without a screen, you’ll be relying on voice commands for much of your interactions with Vive Eagle, and you’ll have the option of working with OpenAI or Gemini out of the box — and can toggle between them as needed — as well as its own Vive AI. A side button lets you call up your agent of choice without needing a wake word, and you can also reconfigure the button in software. HTC includes two years of AI access with your purchase, and there will be monthly plans available after that.
Rather than a charging case, HTC goes with a more flexible pogo-pin cable solution. And for charging on the go, a tiny magnetic battery pack weighing just 8GB can snap right on the frames to fill them back up to 80%. The frame’s electronics are all IP54-certified, keeping them safe against light water exposure.
Privacy is a big deal for smart glasses right now, and HTC is equipping the Vive Eagle with some clever protections because of that. Rather than just letting people know you’re filming by illuminating an LED, HTC also listens for “stop recording” commands from your subject. The glasses also won’t record if you take them off and leave them sitting somewhere.
Pricing starts at $499 for clear or sun lenses, with auto-darkening photochromic lenses available for $599. HTC is taking US orders as of today.

41,2026-09-03,"Last year, a federal judge said Google was illegally monopolizing some ad markets.
This week, we learned about the punishment Google is likely to receive for its sins: Not much at all.
While the details of Judge Leonie M. Brinkema’s order remain under seal for now, we have the broad strokes: It will likely have to play nicer with competitors — but in a way that’s unlikely to have any real impact on its business, and certainly not on its core business. Most crucially, it will not have to engage in any structural changes, like splitting itself apart.
“This is pretty close to the best case scenario for them,” ad tech veteran and author Ari Paparo tells me.
If that sounds familiar, there’s a good reason: In 2024, a different federal judge also ruled that Google ran an illegal monopoly in search. The next year, he announced the company’s punishment — which also barely affected Google’s business, and did not require “structural” changes, like selling off parts of itself.
It’s become a recurring theme for Google and its Big Tech peers: The US Government — starting with the first Trump administration, and even more so in the Biden years — has been taking them to court, in an effort to reduce the reach and clout they’ve accumulated over the last couple decades. And the feds keep losing.
In Google’s case, the government has “won” twice, but its prizes are barely consolation prizes.
In other cases, it has been a straight-up loss: Last year, Meta beat the federal suit accusing it of being a monopoly, which means it didn’t have to sell off Instagram or WhatsApp. And in 2022, the US tried to stop Microsoft from buying gaming giant Activision — and lost that case three years later.
And given that the US government has never made a serious attempt to pass legislation reining in Big Tech — except for 2024’s TikTok sell-or-ban law, which everyone basically decided to ignore — court cases were supposed to be the one tool the feds had left. They haven’t worked.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
The federal effort to slow down Big Tech in the courts isn’t formally over. The government is still appealing its Meta loss. And it still has pending cases against Amazon and Apple.
More important is that there are many other anti-Big Tech court cases.
Meta, for instance, just agreed to pay $18 billion to settle a teen addiction suit brought by several US states. Mark Zuckerberg may reasonably call that settlement a bargain, as my colleague Pranav Dixit argues. But it’s a reminder that Big Tech is still dealing with state suits, civil suits filed by users, and a whole lot of regulatory scrutiny in Europe.
All of which means Big Tech is likely to write more checks in the coming years. But investors seem fine with that — Meta’s stock went up after it announced its potential $18 billion payout; Google stock rose after Wednesday's news.
And that reaction makes sense. These fights are over Big Tech as it used to be — this week’s Google news is about a shrinking part of its ad business that may only account for 1% of revenue today — and not where it wants to go, since none of the suits are about the industry’s headlong plunge into AI.
So maybe Washington and others will be able to make Big Tech pay for the old internet. There’s no sign they will keep the companies from owning the next one.

42,2026-09-03,"Nvidia Goes All-In on Open AI Models, Buys Hugging Face for $12.93 Billion
Search

Nvidia has agreed to acquire New York City-based AI company Hugging Face for a whopping $12,930,300,000. 
Hugging Face, which many describe as a sort of GitHub for AI models, is home to an expansive open-source library of AI tools. Nvidia’s CEO Jensen Huang describes Hugging Face as “a vibrant home for the open model developer community.” 
“More than 18 million developers, researchers and creators use Hugging Face to share more than 3 million models, 500,000 datasets and 1 million applications. More than 200,000 companies use the platform to discover, evaluate, customize and deploy AI,” Huang continues.
Despite spending nearly $13 billion to acquire Hugging Face, Huang says Nvidia will keep Hugging Face alive as an open platform. 
“Hugging Face will continue to support open source and open weight models from across the ecosystem, from every model builder. It will continue to support multi-cloud and multi-accelerator development and deployment, so builders can use the hardware and infrastructure that best fit their work,” Huang promises. 

Nvidia has demonstrated a longstanding commitment to open AI models and has been the largest contributor of open models and data to the Hugging Face community since early 2025. As of today, Nvidia has released more than 500 models and over 250 open datasets on Hugging Face. Many other familiar companies also release open data on Hugging Face, including Meta, Microsoft, and AMD, to name a few. 
Huang says open models enable startups, businesses, universities, and public institutions to build more powerful AI models without training every model entirely from scratch. 
“That is how AI can advance safely, strengthen cybersecurity and sovereignty, accelerate innovation, and reach factories, hospitals, farms, classrooms and Main Street businesses around the world,” Huang explains. 
Nvidia has benefited greatly from the proliferation of AI models and technology, which require the very chips Nvidia makes. In the past five years, propelled significantly by AI growth, Nvidia’s stock price has increased by nearly 900 percent. The company’s market cap has grown from around $300 billion to $5.5 trillion at the time of writing. Nvidia’s stock rose from just shy of $23 per share to nearly $228. The more accessible AI models are to users, the more in demand Nvidia’s chips become.
“As the opportunity for open models accelerates, Hugging Face can serve the global AI community at unprecedented scale. Nvidia’s infrastructure, engineering and global reach can help improve platform reliability, safety, model evaluation, inference and deployment capabilities, while preserving the open ecosystem that made Hugging Face foundational,” Huang says.

43,2026-09-03,"
No tracker on the market ships with an actual leash. Apple’s AirTag is a stud, so you buy a separate keychain loop just to hang it on anything. Chipolo is a disc with a small aperture punched into the edge. Google’s new Pixel Tag is a plain little monolith with the same problem. Samsung and Tile at least had the sense to build in a substantially wide hole or channel so you could pass a carabiner or leash of your own through it, which is basically an admission that they knew the leash was missing and left the job to you. Belkin’s SureFind Loop Cable Tag is the first one that just builds the leash in, and then goes one further: that leash is a working USB-C to USB-C cable you can unclip and use to charge the tag itself, or your phone, or literally anything else with a USB-C port.
It costs $24.99, weighs almost nothing, and the cable snaps back into the tag’s body to top off the 120mAh cell inside once you are done borrowing it. A rep on the IFA show floor in Berlin told me to expect roughly six to nine months of tracking per charge, a number Belkin has not put in writing anywhere yet. The tag itself is a flat white teardrop with one button, a status LED, and it is certified for both Apple Find My and Google Find Hub so it does not care which phone tribe you belong to. It ships in September across the Americas, Australia, New Zealand and Asia Pacific. Europe, where Belkin just showed it off, does not get to buy it at all.
Designer: Belkin






A braided USB-C cable and a fabric carry strap are already built from the same material logic, a length of reinforced cord with a connector at each end, and Belkin’s actual design move was refusing to treat those as two separate problems. The loop threads through a bag handle or backpack zipper, clips shut with a small sliding toggle, and unclips into a functioning charging cable the moment you need one. Belkin has spent years making the loops and holders other brands’ trackers needed just to attach to anything, so the fact that this idea sat unbuilt for that long, inside a company with decades of cable manufacturing behind it, says more about industry habit than about engineering difficulty. Sometimes the obvious fix just needs someone willing to stop selling the workaround.







Using the cable means unclipping the tag from whatever it was hanging on, so the tracker stops tracking the thing it was tied to for as long as you are borrowing the cable to charge a phone at an airport gate. That is a fair compromise for most people most of the time, but Belkin’s marketing does not mention it, and a design this clever should be able to admit its own limits. There is also no published IP rating for this specific model, unlike the SureFind Card, which carries IP68 and can survive a dunk in water. A tag meant for backpacks and luggage sitting on tarmacs in the rain probably deserved that spec sheet line too.

Dual certification for Apple Find My and Google Find Hub means you can buy the tag without worrying about which ecosystem you’re in. However, it’s worth noting that you have to choose one ecosystem to tether your device to. If you’re an Apple guy, you configure it with Find My. If you’re team Android, stick to Google’s offering. Belkin is entering a crowded field days after Apple’s AirTag 2 and ahead of Google’s own Pixel Tag, both of which still lean on separate keychain accessories to attach to anything. Chipolo and Pebblebee already proved rechargeable trackers work and do not need replacement coin cells. Belkin took that idea and folded a second function into the strap itself, a leash that charges things instead of just hanging them. How that holds up outside a Berlin show floor, across the actual months of use it is being asked to survive, is the open question.

You get light from above. You get wind from above too. So at some point, somebody was bound to ask the obvious next question, why…

Horizontal printers are not only an aesthetic vibe-killer in your office, but they also take up way too much space. As you might infer from…
It is said that first impressions matter. We spend hours going over every facet of our product design, trying to achieve the perfect version of…
My camera and I are inseparable during vacation trips. It’s a shame that weather often dictates how and where I use it tho. Like I…

Tech > Mobile > Wearables
With $70 million of investment from companies including Qualcomm, the smart ring-maker is planning its next-gen AI wearables.
Principal Writer
Principal Writer
September 3, 2026, 4:38 pm ET
Smart ring company Ultrahuman is up there with Samsung as the biggest rivals to Oura, but it has some lofty ambitions for its next steps, which it hinted at on Thursday at IFA here in Berlin. The company announced it had raised $70 million from Qualcomm Ventures, Labcorp, Alpha Wave, Blume and others to develop next-generation AI wearables.
The smart ring market is currently dominated by Oura, but Ultrahuman is finding ways to set itself apart. It has already evolved rapidly over the past few years into a health company offering a range of complementary products and services, including blood testing and an ambient sleep monitor that sits by your bed. Earlier this year, it launched the Ring Pro, which has better battery life than its predecessors and also doesn’t require a subscription, unlike some of its rivals.
The new funding is set to further boost this growth, putting the Ultrahuman Ring at the center of an ecosystem where data about your body is used for more insightful and actionable purposes. Ultrahuman plans to invest the money in sensing, materials, electrical engineering, algorithms, physiology and clinical science to build future rings and ultimately make the human body more understandable with AI.
The partnership with Qualcomm draws Ultrahuman into a much wider ecosystem of AI products, many of which are also wearable, with which the Ultrahuman Ring could become interoperable. One example is the Meta Glasses, which the Ultrahuman Ring could be linked to for purposes of gesture control, said Bhuvan Srinivasan, the company’s chief business officer, in an interview.
“What we’re really thinking is you could have a ring that helps you control what’s happening in the glasses, but then at the same time at night it measures all your sleep and all your health,” he said. “So it’s valuable to you both in the day and the night.”
This won’t be possible with the Ring Pro, he added. “There’ll be another Ring level that we are working on with them.”
The partnership with diagnostic and drugs company Labcorp is also important to bringing Ultrahuman’s vision to life, due to the support it can provide to Ultrahuman’s Blood Vision preventative blood-testing service that it’s been building out for the past two years, Srinivasan said.
“The idea behind Blood Vision is really to bring together all the things you cannot measure on a wearable, but you can measure in your blood, which is also very valuable,” he said. “And now we really want to roll that out and make preventive health blood testing much more mainstream. And that’s really why we tied up with them over time.”
The funding could help Ultrahuman position itself as a bridge between people’s health, AI agents and other AI-equipped devices. “Ultrahuman is pioneering a new generation of personal AI devices that make personalized insights effortless,” said Quinn Li, senior vice president of Qualcomm Technologies and global head of Qualcomm Ventures. “We look forward to supporting their journey as they expand the possibilities of personal AI devices.”
Editors’ note: Katie Collins’ travel costs for the IFA conference were covered in part by Xiaomi. The judgments and opinions of CNET are our own.

Tech
Citing rising costs, Microsoft is changing its cloud gaming service options.
Contributing writer
Contributing writer
September 3, 2026, 6:45 pm ET
Playing Doom: The Dark Ages over Xbox Cloud Gaming.
Unlimited Xbox Cloud Gaming is going away. Starting in November, the service will have monthly limits based on the Xbox Game Pass subscription tier, according to a Microsoft post on Thursday. Cloud gaming hours will be capped at:
Microsoft said it’s making these changes due to the increased cost of cloud gaming. “Moving to monthly limits allows us to keep offering the service while continuing to invest in its reliability and performance,” the company said in the post. “We understand that for some players the practical result is a higher cost.”
The limited hours will impact 4% of Xbox Game Pass subscribers, Microsoft said. If you fall into that group, you’ll have the option to purchase more cloud gaming time, and it will also be available to gamers without an Xbox Game Pass subscription.
Also see: Gaming Has Never Been Better. So Why Does It Feel Like the Worst Time to Be a Gamer?
Details about how much additional playtime will cost will be revealed in November, but Microsoft did say cloud gaming features will vary by market. However, it’s unclear whether that means it will be priced differently in certain cities or whether there will be throttling to reduce stream quality.
Microsoft also noted that the service change gives Xbox One owners an opportunity to play Xbox Series X and S games without buying a new console, which has gone up in price due to the sharp rise in hardware costs driven by ongoing component shortages. The Xbox division has been hit with setback after setback, starting in May 2025 with the first price hike for the Xbox Series X and S consoles.
A second jump in price for the console came last October, along with a 50% price increase for Xbox Game Pass the following month. Microsoft did reduce the cost of Game Pass subscriptions this past April, but this move to cut cloud gaming hours shows how it’s trying to continue to reduce costs, even after laying off 1,600 employees in the Xbox division and closing four game studios the company owned in July.
Xbox Cloud Gaming was a central part of Microsoft’s This is an Xbox campaign in late 2024 that pushed the idea of gamers using their phones, tablets, laptops and smart TVs as their Xbox through the Xbox app and cloud gaming. The marketing move was to drive more people to subscribe to Xbox Game Pass while also masking disappointing hardware sales for the Xbox Series consoles.
A lack of a proper universal ecosystem across the board due to the absence of an official Xbox app on some platforms, no universal controller options to play the games and an uneven playing experience contributed to the campaign’s failure. It was cited as offending some Xbox employees after Phil Spencer stepped down as the boss of the Xbox division in February.

48,2026-09-03,"Qatar Airways' Qsuite is legendary, with double beds and quad seating options that have made it one of the most coveted business-class seats in the sky. But the QSuite varies plane to plane, and the subtle yet meaningful differences can change the experience.
When flying Qsuite to or from the US, chances are you'll be on an Airbus A350. As of Summer 2026, the jet serves most of Qatar's dozen US gateways. Only three destinations — Washington, DC, New York-JFK, and Seattle — have regularly scheduled Boeing 777-300ER service.
Across Qatar's global network, though, the two jets are more evenly represented. The 777-300ER, a fleet that averages about 13 years old, remains an integral part of its operations across Europe, Asia, and Oceania. The A350 family's average age is about 7.4 years.
No matter the plane you're on, though, you're likely to have a blast, from its tremendous privacy, luxurious bedding, and soft pajamas to its dine-on-demand meal service and extensive Oryx One entertainment system. And with Qatar rolling out Starlink across both its A350 and Boeing 777 fleets, that experience is only getting better.
But flying QSuite on the two planes back-to-back — first from Casablanca to Doha on the 777 and then Doha to DC on the A350 — I've found 4 subtle differences that make the experience aboard the A350 slightly more pleasant.
The first difference is apparent the second you step on board. The Boeing 777 first entered service nearly 30 years ago, and while Qatar's jets aren't nearly that old, some of the cabin features still reflect that era.
The lighting is one of those: On these jets, you'll be greeted by the harsh airplane lights most of us have become accustomed to. The A350, though, feels dramatically different.
Having come to market 20 years after the 777, it sports a much more sophisticated LED lighting system. On my flight, Qatar put it to good use: when I boarded, the cabin was flooded in the airline's signature purple. That's great for Qatar's branding, but as a passenger, it also feels far warmer, more welcoming, and almost festive — which is how any good business-class flight should feel.
The lighting can also be used to help battle jet lag. Throughout the flight, the crew will change the light to resemble sunrise, sunset, dusk, or nighttime to gently nudge your body clock into your new time zone. The 777 also has some adjustable lighting scenarios, but the system feels far more limited.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
While both jets are among the quietest in the sky, the A350 is noticeably quieter. It's powered by one of the newest turbofans on the market: the Rolls-Royce Trent XWB.
With noise requirements becoming ever stricter, Rolls has future-proofed it and designed it to remain well under the limit.
That's great for people who live near airports, but it's also great for those onboard. Especially toward the front of the A350's cabin, I was able to have conversations at a normal speaking volume without much difficulty.
Unless you're a hardcore aviation geek like me, you might not immediately pick up on this next benefit when you're actually flying. But you'll definitely feel it by the time you arrive at your destination.
Unlike the aluminum fuselage found on aircraft such as the 777, much of the A350's structure is made from carbon-fiber-reinforced composites. Those materials are lighter, which is great for fuel economy, but they're also more fatigue-resistant — allowing Airbus to pressurize the cabin at a lower altitude and maintain higher humidity levels.
The Boeing jet's cabin altitude is around 8,000 feet, while the Airbus is around 6,000 feet — meaning passengers on the Airbus are breathing air pressurized to feel like they're about 2,000 feet lower in elevation. This can also change how full you feel after eating, and sometimes change how things taste at altitude.
And the cabin is less dry, which can make a difference on a long flight and may help with sleep. After my A350 flight, I deplaned feeling noticeably more rested than after my 777 flight, even though the trek was more than twice as long — 15 hours compared to seven.
There were two pieces of tech I really appreciate on board the A350 that are lacking on the 777.
First, the A350's seat-back screen has an HDMI port. Of course, most travelers don't carry around an HDMI cable, but Qatar's crew can provide one upon request. That let me hook up my laptop to the large seatback display and effectively turn it into a second monitor. Pair that with Starlink WiFi, and Qsuite becomes a surprisingly productive workspace at 35,000 feet.
The other feature might be less practical, but it is more fun. The A350 has exterior cameras that passengers can access via the entertainment system, offering views of the aircraft from around the aircraft during the flight.
That means you can watch the scenery below or see the aircraft take off and land, even if you aren't in a window seat. It's a small feature, but one that I always seek out.
While the A350 is a fantastic machine, there is one area where I preferred the Boeing: space. 
The 777's cabin is nearly a foot wider than the A350's, and while that might not sound like much, it makes a noticeable difference. That's especially true if you're trying to move around while service is underway, as the wider aisle makes it much easier to pass others. The suite itself is also a hair wider.
That's not to say the A350 feels cramped. The size of the Qsuite remains generous on this jet, too, but those who prioritize more physical space may prefer the 777.
At the end of the day, if you're in a position where you're choosing between these two planes, you have a very good problem on your hands. Still, I'd pick the A350.
The 777 gives you a little more room, but the A350's quieter cabin, better lighting, more comfortable cabin environment, and additional technology make the overall experience feel noticeably newer and, more importantly, leave you feeling more refreshed at your destination.
Qatar Airways had no comment.

49,2026-09-03,"This summer, Morgan Symone was ready to break up with Stanley. Then Frank Green came along.
We're talking, of course, about water bottle brands.
""I had a Stanley for a while, but she was always leaking, and I'm not a person who will put a charm [cover] on the straw,"" Symone, a 27-year-old from Los Angeles, told Business Insider. ""I wanted to pivot to something new and fresh.""
The recent master's graduate and marketing professional started researching and found the Australian company Frank Green online. She was so intrigued by the brand that she told her friend about it at lunch the next day.
A few weeks later, a personalized, green-and-pink Frank Green bottle arrived on Symone's doorstep — a gift from her friend.
""I don't go anywhere without it,"" Symone said. ""I bring it with me in my car, at work, in my bag, to the beach, to the gym; I love it.""
The cup has become both a practical tool and a fashion accessory for Symone and other fans. And while it's popular, it's not so trendy that you have to fight a crowd to get one — at least, not yet. But with its shiny chrome bottles popping up across social media, Frank Green is starting to look like the next ""it"" bottle for American shoppers.
There's a good chance you already have an Owala, a Stanley cup, and maybe a Bink bottle in your kitchen cabinet. Water bottles have become more than drinkware in recent years. They're status symbols.
Shoppers waited hours in line for coveted Stanley releases, with their prices ranging from $25 to $75. Some Target workers were even fired after buying limited-edition cups themselves, and middle schoolers became ruthless in their quests to have the best bottles.

At its peak popularity, Stanley's annual sales increased from $94 million in 2020 to an estimated $750 million in 2023, largely due to its 40-ounce tumbler, CNBC reported. It also fixed that leaking issue Symone mentioned by releasing a new version of the cup, the Quencher ProTour, with a flip-top straw.
Over time, Owala, whose prices range from $26 to $55, emerged as another trendy option, wooing ""cool girls"" with its FreeSip bottle, which has a top loop for carrying and a built-in straw. Today, rare or sold-out Owalas get listed for triple the price — sometimes over $100 — on sites like eBay.
Joe Derochowski, a home industry advisor at the global market research company Circana, told Business Insider that sales of reusable drinkware have remained strong since 2020, with $5.2 billion in portable beverage items sold over the past year, up from $3.1 billion in 2024.
Frank Green isn't a response to the water bottle craze, but the Australian brand said it still wants to earn a spot on American shelves.
The brand offers customizable bottles with metal straws and rubber straps attached to the lids, making them easy to carry. But its shiny chrome bottles are what's really capturing people's attention across social media, offering a more elevated aesthetic than many of the printed or brightly colored options that have dominated the market for years. ""There's a visual aspect and the quality is so good,"" Symone said.
One complaint from Symone, however, is that the bottle doesn't fit inside car cupholders, which is a big deal. Taking reusable cups on the go is kind of the whole point. No one wants their bottle rolling around their passenger seat while they drive.
It's a drawback Frank Green has attempted to address by offering a $15.95 cupholder expander on its website.
Frank Green may be unfamiliar to some US shoppers, but the brand isn't new.
Benjamin Young, the brand's CEO, founded the company in 2013 as a retailer that could bring both sustainability and style to customers. He first crafted a reusable coffee cup and later introduced ceramic-lined water bottles.
Now, the brand sells the latter alongside kids' cups, water bottle holders, and other products. Its most popular products are its range of chrome bottles, which cost $47.95 for a 20-ounce bottle and $52.95 for a 34-ounce version.
Frank Green experienced significant growth in Australia. Nearly six years into its business, the company was generating over 15 million Australian dollars, or more than $10 million, in annual revenue, The Sydney Morning Herald reported.
Frank Green declined to share current revenue and sales numbers with Business Insider.
""We've always thought of Frank Green as a global brand, not just an Aussie one, so the US felt like the natural next step,"" Young, who announced a US expansion back in 2018, told Business Insider. ""What's been amazing for us is seeing Americans get on board with what we're about: You shouldn't have to choose between sustainability and functionality for style.""
Social media is helping the brand reach more shoppers. In fact, the brand had a TikTok presence before it even joined TikTok. According to a business case study by the social media company, videos about Frank Green's products surged in 2022, prompting the company to create its own account.
""Most Americans have discovered us through social media, off the back of the 'emotional support water bottle' trend,"" Young said.
Now, Frank Green is seeing another wave of attention online. While reporting this story, we watched dozens of videos that new American fans have posted about their Frank Green water bottles. Again and again, they described the bottles as ""chic."" US Google search interest in ""Frank Green bottle"" has also surged in 2026.
And in August, Frank Green checked off another signature sign of American success: a collaboration. It launched two $80 bottles with the skincare brand Laneige.
Even though Frank Green has yet to catch up to the popularity of its US competitors, that fact has become a selling point for some consumers.
""Frank Green is still a little niche — and I like that,"" Symone said.
No comments right now, check back later.

50,2026-09-03,"Our work with IDRX and the MoU with Nobu Bank, which supports our rebalancing operations in Indonesia, offer an early look at how local-currency stablecoins, domestic banking infrastructure, liquidity, and onchain settlement can come together and scale across Asia.
Across Asia, issuers are bringing local currencies onchain, regulators are establishing digital asset frameworks, and financial institutions are exploring stablecoins for payments, treasury, and cross-border settlement.
Issuance, however, is only the first layer of a functioning financial network.
Moving local currencies between countries requires banking connectivity, institutional liquidity, foreign exchange execution, compliance, and settlement infrastructure. Without these components working together, stablecoins remain isolated digital assets rather than part of usable settlement and FX infrastructure.
Indonesia offers opportunities to demonstrate how those layers can operate as a complete corridor and be replicated across Asia.
Dollar-backed stablecoins demonstrated that fiat-denominated value can move globally, settle around the clock, and integrate with programmable financial infrastructure.
In Asia, existing payment flows often introduce unnecessary foreign exchange conversions and operational complexity before funds reach their final destination.
Local-currency stablecoins create a more direct path by making currencies such as IDR, SGD, JPY, KRW, and THB available within digital settlement networks.
The next phase of stablecoin adoption depends on infrastructure that allows those currencies to move efficiently between markets.
Indonesia is one of Asia’s largest digital economies, with expanding regional trade, widespread adoption of digital financial services, and an active fintech ecosystem.
Rupiah-backed digital assets are establishing the building blocks for onchain financial activity.
IDRX brings the Indonesian rupiah into blockchain-based environments through assets designed to maintain a one-to-one relationship with IDR. IDRX is deployed on Kaia, where I also serve as Chief Stablecoin Officer alongside my role at Ratio.
In addition, we have signed an MoU with Nobu Bank, which supports our rebalancing operations in Indonesia.
Ratio is working with these participants to expand the IDR corridor and prepare infrastructure for institutional adoption.
The corridor is designed to validate the complete transaction lifecycle from entering the ecosystem in IDR to accessing liquidity, executing FX, settling across borders, and returning value through domestic banking rails.
That lifecycle establishes the foundation for institutional payment infrastructure.
Financial institutions require an infrastructure stack where multiple layers operate together.
Users must be able to enter and exit the stablecoin reliably at its intended value. Transparent reserve management, minting, and redemption processes establish confidence in the underlying asset.
Domestic banks provide the fiat access points that connect digital assets with regulated financial systems.
Payment providers require sufficient liquidity to execute meaningful transaction volumes with predictable pricing and minimal market impact.
Cross-border payments depend on efficient pricing and conversion between local currencies. Reliable FX execution becomes a critical component of institutional settlement.
Every corridor must support the regulatory, operational, and reporting requirements of the jurisdictions it connects.
The final layer connects issuers, banks, PSPs, fintechs, wallets, and enterprise platforms into usable settlement and FX infrastructure.
Each layer contributes to a complete settlement infrastructure.
An orchestration layer coordinates these components through a common integration framework, allowing institutions to access multiple participants without building separate connections for every issuer, bank, liquidity provider, and settlement network.
Through our work with IDRX and Nobu Bank, the IDR corridor is now live and integrated. Ratio is currently working toward executing its first commercial transaction volumes, supporting workflows including:
This deployment is designed to ensure that regulated institutions can execute these transactions repeatedly, predictably, and at commercially relevant volumes, establishing an operational model that can be extended beyond Indonesia.
Asia’s financial infrastructure will continue to reflect the diversity of its markets.
Each country maintains its own currency, regulatory framework, banking system, and domestic financial institutions. Those differences make localized implementation essential while creating an opportunity for shared regional infrastructure.
Each new corridor would introduce local issuers, domestic banking partners, liquidity providers, and regulatory requirements.
The underlying operational architecture, however, can remain consistent.
The core infrastructure layers do not need to be rebuilt each time a new market is added. A shared orchestration layer allows compliant local ecosystems to connect while preserving their existing financial infrastructure.
The result is a network of interoperable local corridors rather than a single regional stablecoin, and each successful corridor strengthens the foundation for the next.
At Ratio, we are building this orchestration layer for Asia. By coordinating liquidity, foreign exchange, settlement, and compliance across multiple jurisdictions, we are creating an operational framework that enables regional payment corridors, without requiring markets to abandon their local financial systems.
As more local-currency stablecoins enter production, the ability to connect them efficiently will become increasingly important.
Indonesia represents the beginning of that model.
From there, Asia’s stablecoin network can grow one corridor at a time: locally regulated, institutionally connected, and built on interoperable infrastructure.

John Cho serves as the CEO and Co-Founder of Ratio and the Chief Stablecoin Officer (CSO) at the Kaia DLT Foundation. Concurrently, his leadership at Kaia focuses on driving real-world stablecoin use cases, ecosystem partnerships, and scalable adoption across key Asian markets. His diverse professional foundation also includes strategic planning roles in the energy sector, notably at SK, as well as experience in investment banking.
