51,2026-09-03,"Brockman told reporters that Astra qualifies as artificial general intelligence and that the US government came back with nothing to change, but the framework he is describing is voluntary and expressly disclaims preclearance, and the Commission's own evaluation powers only begin once a model is on the market
SAN FRANCISCO, CALIFORNIA – OCTOBER 03: OpenAI Co-founder/Chairman & CTO Greg Brockman speaks onstage during TechCrunch Disrupt San Francisco 2019 at Moscone Convention Center on October 03, 2019 in San Francisco, California. (Photo by Steve Jennings/Getty Images for TechCrunch)
OpenAI’s president says artificial general intelligence has arrived. Greg Brockman told reporters that the company’s new Astra model qualifies, the Washington Post reported.
OpenAI defines AGI as systems that are generally smarter than humans. “I do leave it up to the reader to decide for themselves if this qualifies for them“, Brockman said. “I think we’re there.”
The model went to business users in the Daybreak programme on Thursday. A version carrying added cybersecurity guardrails is planned for paid ChatGPT subscribers, Bloomberg reported.
Both versions block access to the most advanced cyber capabilities. OpenAI has said Astra reached its critical cybersecurity threshold. Sam Altman said the release took longer than hoped.
That is the classification behind the model OpenAI slowed in August. A model reaches it when it can find and build working zero-day exploits against hardened systems with no human help.
Astra has been trained to use computers the way people do. OpenAI says it can handle financial modelling, tax preparation and video game development with minimal human oversight.
The US government looked at it before release. Brockman said there was nothing they came back saying you need to change. He offered that as reassurance.
That is a weaker statement than it sounds.
June’s executive order lets developers give agencies access for up to 30 days before a broad release, and expressly says nothing in it authorises mandatory licensing, preclearance or permitting.
Participation is voluntary, and the NSA director decides which models count as covered frontier models through a classified benchmarking process. No approval was withheld because none was on offer.
Europe does not pre-clear a model either. The Commission gained power to conduct evaluations on 2 August, triggered by insufficient documentation or an alert from its scientific panel. It can demand access through APIs or source code.
Those are post-market powers rather than gates. Separately, from 11 September, manufacturers of products with digital elements must report actively exploited vulnerabilities within 24 hours of becoming aware.
So nobody cleared this model in either jurisdiction. OpenAI has been rewriting its framework since the Hugging Face breach, and its own classification remains the binding one.

52,2026-09-03,"The widow of a farmer who died after he fell into a slurry pit has spoken about the stresses facing workers after a rise in deaths.
Margaret Owen said the impact of husband Islwyn's death had been brought back to her once again following reports that a father and son had died in similar circumstances last week.
She said Islwyn had worried about bad weather and recently-introduced rules on slurry spreading when he had fallen at his farm in Bala, Gwynedd, in 2024.
Latest figures from the Health and Safety Executive (HSE) show 22 workers died on British farms during 2025-26 - the highest number in a decade.
Margaret said the number of deaths had made it appear ""things are getting worse and worse"".
An inquest into Islwyn's death found he had fallen into the slurry pit after he had climbed on to a short wall to adjust some machinery at Cefn Bodig farm in Llanycil, Bala.
He suffered asphyxia along with serious injuries to his right arm and chest caused by the machinery, and his body was found by a neighbour 90 minutes after he was reported missing on the evening of 4 September 2024.
Islwyn was pronounced dead at the scene, and his death was ruled as accidental.
Margaret said that, although it had not been the cause of his death, Islwyn had been under pressure because of a combination of bad weather and the Welsh government's rules that banned farmers from spreading slurry between mid-October to January.
She said that farmers in general are often under a lot of stress.
The ban on muck-spreading over winter months to protect rivers is something that Wales' environment minister Llyr Gruffydd has said he wanted to change after Plaid Cymru came into power.
""The weather had been wet and there was a problem with mixing the slurry and emptying the slurry before the cattle came in - it was quite a stressful situation,"" she told BBC Cymru Fyw.
The deaths of Alan Ford, 72, and his son Rick, 42, after they had fallen into an industrial slurry tank at a farm in Chester on 26 August, had brought back memories of her husband's death, she said.
The 22 deaths of workers on farms in Wales, England and Scotland during 2025-26 was the highest in a decade, and second only to the number of deaths of those working in construction (27), according to HSE figures.
But the rate of farm deaths was seven times higher per 100,000 workers, and 21 times more likely than the all-industry average.
""It seems that things are getting worse and worse,"" Margaret said, and that ""four [people] within four miles of here have died in recent years - it's scary isn't it?""
""Islwyn was so careful - one of those rare farmers who wore a helmet,"" she said.
""He was aware of the dangers of farming and I kept saying 'be careful'.""
She said she had known something was wrong on the day Islwyn died ""because he was never very late for dinner"", and said the nature of his death had been ""hard to accept"".
Margaret had to sell their farm stock and arrange for other farmers to rent the majority of their land.
""For over a year I had tears coming every day, and I thought I'm going to be like this forever. But it's getting easier,"" she said.
She had stayed in Llanycil and said it had been ""important to focus on the nice memories"", keeping herself busy by keeping horses.
An Oxfordshire farmer says his Brussels sprouts are under threat from pests after long hot summer.
The event will take place at Newbury Showground, in Berkshire, next year. 
Bluetongue has hit farmers hard and they have dreaded seeing the impact on their livestock.
Farmers in Somerset and Gloucestershire say this year's heatwaves have affected the size of their produce.
The Royal Agricultural Benevolent Institution will roll out the support from 7 September.

Forget building AI from a Silicon Valley office. One of tech's fastest growing jobs is sending workers directly to customers to configure AI tools, integrate software, and solve complex business problems.
Job postings for ""forward-deployed"" engineers rose more than 1000% between January and August 2026, compared to the same period last year—and more than 4,600% compared to 2023, according to Lightcast data. That far outpaces the broader tech job market, where postings grew 13% year over year. 
Similar growth has been reported on LinkedIn and Indeed, according to The New York Times.
The concept isn't entirely new. Palantir has long been known for its forward-deployed model, in which technical employees work closely with customers to build and implement software in the field. 
Now, as companies race to effectively deploy AI into business processes, that model is spreading like wildfire.
""Companies are increasingly tapping into powerful AI models and struggling to turn those models into something that actually works inside of their business, as connecting them to proprietary data, existent systems and specific workflows can be a big roadblock to overcome, Paul Farnsworth, president of tech career platform Dice, told Fortune. ""Forward-deployed engineers can help fill that gap.""
And the jobs come with a substantial paycheck. Lightcast data shared by Farnsworth found that the median advertised salaries for forward-deployed engineers was more than $188,000—compared to roughly $145,000 for traditional software engineers. 
At some tech giants like Anthropic, compensation for some forward-deployed roles can even reach $400,000.
Palantir currently has around four dozen open forward-deployed positions, mostly in software development. Candidates have the opportunity to apply for client-specific opportunities, including major corporations like Intel, defense organizations like NATO, and governments like Norway.
""As an FDSE, your responsibilities look similar to those of a startup CTO: you'll work in small teams with minimal supervision and own end-to-end execution of high stakes projects,"" a Palantir job listing said. ""Your day might span discussing architecture with fellow engineers, wrangling massive-scale data, coding a custom web app, speaking with customer executives, or establishing strategy for your team.""
That hands-on approach has become a defining part of Palantir's business model—and one the company credits with helping it compete against much larger technology companies.
""We hired the best software engineers in the world, ejected them from the comfort of a Palo Alto office, and dropped them in remote locations to spend their days in the service of incredibly skilled but non-technical operators,"" Meline von Brentano, Palantir's head of digital transformation strategy, wrote in a blog post last month. ""In the process, we beat out much better resourced companies in the race for the best talent.""
And by many metrics, it's been successful. Palantir's market cap now sits at over $400 billion, and in the company's most recent quarterly earnings, the tech firm reported revenue increases of 93% year-over-year, totaling $1.94 billion.
Many of its competitors have begun adopting the same playbook. 
Microsoft, Meta, Google, OpenAI and Anthropic currently all have openings for forward-deployed engineering roles. Others, ranging from established giants like Nvidia to startup unicorns like Scale AI, have also adopted the ""forward-deployed"" model for other job categories, too, including in product management and tech architecture.
Becoming a forward-deployed engineer isn't easy. The role requires a mix of technical expertise—including programming, machine learning, generative AI, and tech infrastructure—paired with the consulting, communication, and leadership skills needed to work directly with customers.
For those interested in landing a forward-deployed engineering role, Farnsworth said it's critical to start with modern tech fundamentals and then build expertise in APIs, data pipelines, cloud infrastructure, and how to get AI systems reliably into production. From there, workers should lean into the softer skills like problem-solving, communication, and business judgment.
""Just knowing how to use the latest model or AI tool isn't enough anymore. The bigger differentiator is being able to connect that technical knowledge to a business problem,"" Farnsworth said. ""Can you walk into an ambiguous situation, understand how a workflow actually operates, communicate with technical and nontechnical stakeholders and build something that creates a measurable result?""
Workers already in tech can build that expertise in their current roles by finding opportunities to put AI into real business processes.
""I'd also encourage tech professionals to look for opportunities in their current roles to deploy AI into real workflows and document the impact—whether that's revenue generated, time saved, errors reduced or a process improved,"" Farnsworth added. ""Focusing there will help those looking for forward-deployed roles be competitive and land the job.""

Snap is under pressure. Its shares are down more than 30% this year.
Copied to clipboard
Snap is preparing to introduce a pair of $2,195 smart glasses on September 16. CEO Evan Spiegel poured years and significant resources into the glasses, which he is eager to introduce, according to a new report from The Wall Street Journal.
Ahead of the company’s fall product launch, Spiegel appeared on CNBC in June. Sources told the Journal that at least one colleague urged him not to wear the glasses for the entire interview, suggesting he talk about the product rather than wear it on camera throughout the segment. Despite receiving this advice, Spiegel chose to wear the glasses for the whole interview anyway. 
The reaction online was swift. Social media users called the glasses “laughably ugly” and compared them to “3D glasses at an IMAX theater.” “Are we being punked?” one poster asked, per the Journal.
Spiegel has championed the project for years. The Journal reported that Spiegel’s focus on smart glasses has frustrated employees and investors at times, especially as Snap’s share price has struggled. 
His commitment has prompted questions inside the company. According to the Journal, some leaders have raised concerns about how much time Spiegel is devoting to the glasses compared to Snapchat, the company’s core revenue-driver. 
Still, Spiegel views the device as a long-term wager on how AI could reshape people’s relationship with technology. Additionally, some analysts see the smart glasses as a possible long-term way for Snap to reduce its reliance on its flagship app.
Snap’s September debut of its $2,195 Specs glasses will be a critical test of Spiegel’s long-term vision for the company and its ability to find growth opportunities beyond Snapchat. 
The company was once one of social media’s hottest names, but it has struggled to become profitable while facing deep-pocketed rivals such as TikTok and Instagram parent Meta. Meanwhile, Meta is pursuing its own strategy around AI smart glasses, with prices for its Ray-Ban Meta smart glasses starting at $379. Meta has sold more than two million pairs since debuting the glasses in October 2023. 
Snap’s stock has fallen more than 30% this year, and the 15-year-old company has yet to post an annual profit. According to the Journal, over the past three years, it has lost nine million daily users in North America for its core social media app.
Snap has also experienced substantial executive turnover. In the past 18 months, its general counsel, chief financial officer, chief creative officer, chief communications officer and the executive overseeing the smart glasses’ augmented-reality operations have all left.
A Snap spokesperson told the Journal that revenue rose 19% from a year earlier in the latest quarter and that the platform was nearing one billion monthly active users. The spokesperson also said Spiegel’s CNBC interview boosted awareness of the product, while his X following has quadrupled since the appearance.
“We have invested thoughtfully in making computing more human with Specs augmented reality glasses and we are looking forward to sharing more at our September 16th launch event,” the spokesperson said.

Mivi Android

Last month, Indian electronics brand Fire-Boltt entered the Indian smartphone market with the launch of the Boltt Ace 5G and Boltt Evo 4G smartphones. This month, another Indian electronics brand, Mivi, will enter the Indian smartphone market with its first smartphone, the Mivi One 5G.
The Mivi One 5G will be powered by a Snapdragon chip and will be sold online exclusively through Flipkart.
One name, zero compromises!#MiviOne pic.twitter.com/PYOcTYM1NY
The teasers Mivi posted on its official X account suggest the Mivi One 5G will have a flat back panel, with green being one of the color options, while Flipkart revealed the smartphone will run ""clean software.""
Beyond that, neither Mivi nor Flipkart has divulged anything about the Mivi One 5G. However, expect to hear more from Mivi about the Mivi One 5G as we inch closer to its launch.
In this price range no one really cares about bloatware.  

There are lots of options going here at every price point, both stock and custom android. It'll be hard to compete with moto, which is the consumer's favourite for stock android at budget prices (below 25k INR) 
 
But let's see what ...
Every budget phone looks similar on a spec sheet, L.O.R.D. The differentiation lies in the software ecosystem and localized pricing. A clean Android build without the usual adware found in this tier is a massive difference for actual users.

	Directed by Safy Nebbou and sold internationally by France TV Distribution, “Our Missing Sons” is based on the novel “Les Disparus des Argonnes” by Julie Pey, who also penned the script along with Nebbou and Mary Arnaud.

					Here's How to Watch Moses Itauma vs. Filip Hrgovic Boxing Pay-Per-View Live Online		
			














	
		
					Alex Kingston, Iain Glen, Mimi Keene and Arian Nik Join Ray Winstone in Prime Video Action Comedy Series 'Danger Money' (EXCLUSIVE)		
			








	The four-part series, produced by Studio 14 Films, follows Jocelyne Veyrades (played by Alix Poisson) as she grapples with the mysterious disappearance of her son Gilles while on leave from the military. Convinced that he did not simply desert, as his military superiors and local police assume, she begins her own investigation, only to discover that other young men serving in the military have also gone missing in the region. Yet Jocelyne keeps hitting a wall of indifference from the military and authorities. Despite the obstacles, she joins forces with other families and takes her case to the public.




			Popular on Variety		






	“‘Our Missing Sons’ stands out through the strength of its writing, the precision of its direction, and the exceptional performance of Alix Poisson, who previously starred in Jean-Xavier de Lestrade’s ‘Those Who Lived’ and ‘Sambre,’” said Julia Schulte, France TV Distribution’s director of international sales.  

	“Inspired by true events and set in the 1980s, the series resonates powerfully with many of today’s societal issues. With striking realism, what begins as a seemingly ordinary criminal investigation gradually unfolds into a much deeper exploration of social and family taboos surrounding the sexual abuse of men. At its heart lies the relentless struggle of a mother who refuses to give up until the truth is revealed, standing against institutions largely ruled by men. It is a universal story that will speak to buyers and audiences around the world.”

	










	Having a woman and mother as the protagonist offered a different and welcomed perspective, Nebbou told Variety.

	“I loved the idea of putting this woman at the center of the story. She has something of an Erin Brockovich quality to her. She’s a fighter. She’s stubborn, determined, and she refuses to accept what she’s being told.

	“The army says her son has deserted. But for her, that’s simply impossible. She knows her son. And she holds on to this very simple, almost instinctive belief: A son doesn’t just abandon his mother. So in a way, the investigation is born from that conviction. Everyone else is telling her to accept the official version and move on, and she’s the one person who says, ‘No. Something is wrong.’ And that’s where our story really begins,” the writer-director said.

	Peyr’s book was inspired by the real case of the disappeared of Mourmelon, in which a number of young men, most of them military conscripts, went missing over several years in the 1980s.

	“We were very much inspired by the Mourmelon case, particularly by the general dynamics of what happened, but we also moved quite far away from the actual facts,” Nebbou said.

	“We took that reality as a starting point, and then invented a great deal. The same is true of Julie Peyr’s novel.

	“It was really the initial spark for the project, but as we developed the series, we gradually freed ourselves both from the real case and from the book, and began to create our own story, with our own characters and twists.”

	The series’ cast includes Mehdi Nebbou, Vincent Deniard, Anne Charrier, Cyril Lecomte, Félix Moati, Camille Claris, Xaverine Lefevre and Isabelle Côte-Willem.  

	“Our Missing Sons” is among France TV Distribution’s top titles at the Unifrance Rendez-Vous, which runs Sept. 7-10.

62,2026-09-03,"Merck’s stock is the top performer in its group, but its profitability sits at the very bottom of the pack, forcing a hard question on investors.
Pharmaceutical giant Merck (MRK) is best known for its cancer blockbuster KEYTRUDA, but lately, its stock has been the main event. Shares have climbed +84% over the last twelve months, a run that has handily outpaced the S&P 500’s +19.1% gain. That performance puts Merck at the very top of its competitive class. The problem is, its underlying business results sit near the bottom.
While the company’s headline P/E of 116.7 is distorted by one-off acquisition charges and write-downs weighing on trailing GAAP earnings, even its adjusted P/E tells a demanding story. The question remains whether the market has correctly priced in a major future for Merck, or simply pushed the stock ahead of its underlying earnings power.
Among its closest rivals, Merck’s +84% one-year stock return is first in a group of six. Yet its operational metrics tell a different story. The company’s revenue grew 4.6% over the last twelve months, placing it fourth in the group and well behind the 8.1% growth at Johnson & Johnson. It’s a fraction of the 50% expansion posted by Eli Lilly.
The gap is even starker on profitability. Merck’s 10.5% operating margin ranks dead last among its peers. That figure is less than a third of Eli Lilly’s 50% margin and less than half of the 27% margin Johnson & Johnson delivered over the same period. The market is rewarding Merck with a top-tier return for bottom-tier efficiency and middle-of-the-pack growth.
The market’s enthusiasm isn’t based on last year’s results, but on the promise of the next decade. Management is pointing to a pipeline with a potential “greater than $70 billion of commercial opportunity” from over 20 new products. This is the narrative fueling the stock. Recent events have added tangible proof points, including the FDA approval for LIPFENDRA, the “first and only oral PCSK9 inhibitor” for high cholesterol.
Beyond that, the company announced positive Phase III results for its cancer therapy sac-TMT and its immunology drug tulisokibart. Investors are betting this wave of innovation will successfully navigate the eventual patent expiration of KEYTRUDA. While that patent cliff remains a critical hurdle, the bull case rests on the idea that recent clinical milestones have substantially de-risked the pipeline meant to replace it.
The strongest argument against this optimism is the immense execution risk ahead. A pipeline projected at greater than $70 billion in commercial opportunity is not the same as cash in the bank. The transition away from KEYTRUDA remains the company’s defining challenge, and launching multiple potential blockbusters simultaneously is a monumental task. While analysts have pressed management on the pace of initial access for LIPFENDRA, the broader challenge remains converting early formulary coverage into rapid adoption in a market known for its inertia.
The debate boils down to commercial execution. The pipeline’s potential is clear, but turning that potential into profit is where the risk lies. The first, most visible test of Merck’s ability to deliver on its post-KEYTRUDA vision is happening now with the launch of LIPFENDRA. Management’s goal extends beyond competing with existing drugs; it aims to achieve broad “market expansion” for a new class of oral therapy.
For investors, what bears watching is the early adoption and sales trajectory of LIPFENDRA. Its performance over the next several quarters will provide the first concrete data point on whether Merck can convert its pipeline promise into financial reality. It will be a powerful signal as to whether the market has this story right, or if the stock’s stellar run was premature.
This piece pulled one thread; our full peer-by-peer dashboards for MRK lay every metric side by side, updated daily.
Prefer the theme to this single name? A healthcare ETF like XLV holds the sector rather than this one name. It is still a concentrated bet on that one theme, though, which is exactly the gap the portfolio below closes.
Whichever name wins a peer comparison, buying it concentrates you in one company and one industry, and industries move together: when the group catches a cold, the best house on the block still sneezes.
The Trefis High Quality (HQ) Portfolio diversifies across roughly 30 quality names in different industries, selected on fundamentals and re-balanced with discipline, so no single group’s weather decides the outcome. It has a track record of outpacing a benchmark that combines the three major indices – the S&P 500, S&P Mid-cap, and Russell 2000. Use the comparison to understand the stock; use the portfolio to own the market’s best.

63,2026-09-03,"Srini Gopalan is selling home internet and AI as the growth story after a 25% share slide, while Elliott Investment Management has built a stake in parent Deutsche Telekom to stop a combination that would move the holding company out of Germany and dilute Berlin and KfW below 25%
T-Mobile Arena in Las Vegas
T-Mobile US chief executive Srini Gopalan declined to comment on a possible merger with parent Deutsche Telekom, days after Elliott built a stake to oppose it. A full combination would put the holding company outside Germany and cut the German state and KfW’s joint holding from about 28% to roughly 17%.
T-Mobile US chief executive Srini Gopalan is selling home internet and AI as the growth story. He told Bloomberg he will not manage the company to a quarterly share price, Bloomberg reported.
The shares are down 25% since he was named to the job last September. Second-quarter revenue missed and subscriber growth fell 13% year on year.
He would not discuss the merger. Elliott Investment Management has built a stake in parent Deutsche Telekom and wants the plan dropped in favour of buybacks.
That is the story sitting inside the interview. Deutsche Telekom owns just over 53% of T-Mobile US.
The German government owns about 14% of Deutsche Telekom. The state development bank KfW owns roughly another 14%.
The proposal is a full combination rather than a buyout. A new holding company would make an all-share bid for both and list on both sides of the Atlantic.
It would probably be incorporated outside Germany, possibly in Ireland, on the model of the 2018 Linde and Praxair merger.
That is what makes it a German political question rather than a telecoms one. A full merger would cut the combined state holding to roughly 17%, below the 25% that carries a blocking minority under German company law.
It also needs 75% shareholder approval, and support has been draining. T-Mobile’s own executives told Deutsche Telekom in August that they no longer support it.
Minority shareholders had argued the deal undervalued the American business. T-Mobile generates around $18B in adjusted free cash flow a year and pays more than $2B in dividends to Bonn.
Deutsche Telekom has kept buying in Europe regardless. It took Macquarie’s Polish fibre and TV assets in a roughly €1B deal.
Its answer this week was management. A new chief financial officer from April 2027 and a chief technology officer arriving from Amazon Web Services in November, while T-Mobile’s next finance chief comes from Shell.
None of that touches the strategic question. Europe’s largest telecoms group is still deciding whether its most profitable asset belongs outside its own jurisdiction, in a market where regulators are being asked to tighten their grip on operators rather than loosen it.

64,2026-09-03,"Broadcom just posted AI chip growth that dwarfs nearly every semiconductor result this year, yet the stock sold off anyway. The real question is whether the market priced in the wrong number entirely.
Read More: Learn 7 ways to generate income with a $1,000,000+ portfolio (sponsor)
This post may contain links from our sponsors and affiliates, and Flywheel Publishing may receive compensation for actions taken through them.
Broadcom (NASDAQ:AVGO | AVGO Price Prediction) reported a quarter that, on the numbers, looks like one of the most consequential AI results of the year. AI semiconductor revenue reached $16.7 billion, up 221% year-on-year and 54% sequentially, and management raised the fiscal 2027 AI revenue outlook to roughly $115 billion, with fiscal 2028 pointing to $230 billion.
The post-earnings reaction was still negative, largely because the Q4 total revenue guide of about $34.8 billion landed close to, but not clearly above, consensus. The settled session closed down 0.66% at $367.24, with the sharper move happening in extended hours around the report.
Revenue came in at $29.591 billion, up 85.5% year-over-year, with non-GAAP EPS of $3.32 against a $3.2382 estimate. Operating margin expanded 240 basis points to 67.9%, and free cash flow was $13.665 billion, or 46% of revenue.
The market chose to trade the one line that disappointed: the Q4 top-line guide. That is the reasonable-sounding overreaction, because the disclosure that mattered more was the forward AI trajectory.
A custom AI accelerator, or XPU, is silicon co-designed with a specific hyperscaler for a specific model family. XPUs represented 73% of AI revenue and XPU shipments grew more than three and a half times year-on-year. Hock Tan put it directly: “When you co-develop a chip that is optimized for your particular LLM workloads, you will outperform any GPU.”
NVIDIA (NASDAQ:NVDA) sells a general-purpose platform with the CUDA ecosystem attached. Broadcom plays a different role, supplying design expertise and networking for hyperscalers who already know what they want to run.
Management named the deployments. Anthropic is deploying one gigawatt of Ironwood in 2026, another five gigawatts of TPU v8i in 2027, and a further 10 gigawatts in 2028. OpenAI has Jalapeno on track for 1.3 gigawatts in 2027 and over five gigawatts across generations in 2028. Gigawatts indicate ordered capacity that still depends on power, land, and packaging before it converts to revenue. The concentration is real: six XPU customers, with four expected to be particularly large. Any one of them changing plans matters.
The underappreciated piece is networking. Tomahawk 6 is a first-to-market 100-terabit switch deployed by pretty much all AI hyperscalers building XPUs with Broadcom, and Tomahawk 7 is taped out as the industry’s first 200-terabit-per-second Ethernet switch. The mix shift toward custom silicon dilutes blended gross margin because memory content is heavier, which is why Q4 gross margin is guided to about 73%. That is a feature. A hyperscaler that co-designed a chip cannot move that program without restarting a multi-year design cycle.


NVIDIA still owns general-purpose accelerated computing and the developer ecosystem, trading at a 45x P/E. Marvell (NASDAQ:MRVL) is the other merchant custom-silicon option but at a smaller scale. Broadcom’s forward P/E near 20x, against a $525.97 analyst target, and Tan’s stated goal of exceeding $30 in EPS in fiscal 2028 make the risk-reward profile attractive despite concentration.
The framing of an Nvidia alternative is the whole point, and we cataloged the early traits the biggest tech winners shared in a free playbook here: The Next Nvidia Playbook. The setup screens as attractive on a research basis, though the customer-concentration discount argues for measured position sizing.

Home > Kitchen & Household > Vacuum Cleaners
Eufy’s sterilization-focused robot vacuum is the product I'm most looking forward to checking out in Berlin.
Smart Home & Robotics Editor
Smart Home & Robotics Editor
September 3, 2026, 4:47 am ET
Anker has made some of our favorite and most innovative robot vacuums over the years, and recently, they’ve also been getting into robot lawn mowers, earning spots on our tested lists. The newest models they’re showing off at IFA Berlin 2026 include a robot vacuum and a robot mower, both with key improvements and features over previous generations. 
Anker’s newest robot vacuum — and potentially the last one to keep the Eufy name (more on that below) — is the Omni E35, and it just might be what my three asthmatic cats need because it focuses on sterilization and disinfection. Anker describes it as “baby-grade,” meaning it’s TÜV Rheinland-certified for the “Maternal and Infant Cleaning Robot mark.” It can eliminate bacteria and dust mites, and it has low noise and high efficiency when it comes to cleaning and self-cleaning.   
Anker says it’s the first and only robot vacuum to earn the Asthma & Allergy Friendly Certification from the Asthma and Allergy Foundation of America. This is especially interesting to me as someone who has the bad luck of having three cats diagnosed with asthma. The vet told me it was incredibly unlikely to occur naturally, so the cause is probably environmental — dust mites, dander, outdoor pollution, fragrances or smoke from the neighbors downstairs. At least a couple of those factors could be addressed by a robot vacuum capable of more thorough fine-dust pickup and sterilization, so I’m interested in seeing it in action and trying it at home.
It has all the typical flagship features you’d expect from a top-tier model, including 35,000 pascals of suction and the Hydro Jet 2.0 self-cleaning roller mop. It also has a kitchen deep clean mode that uses an algorithm to determine how to tackle tougher stains, by saturating, soaking and then scrubbing. 
The Omni E35 will be available starting Sept. 25, though pre-orders will start Sept. 4. It’ll cost $1,100 at MSRP, but if you buy it during the early bird pricing, you can get it for $900. 
Also new in the robotics space is the Eufy S2 Max, Anker’s first robot mower developed entirely in-house. It has vision-based SLAM navigation and real-time 3D mapping, meaning no boundary wires or RTK beacons are required, which has become increasingly common in the robot mower market. It also has improved computing and full all-wheel drive, letting it handle terrain up to 80% slopes, a dual blade system and edge-to-edge cutting with an extendable trimming arm.  
US availability and pricing are still to be determined, but it’ll be available in EU markets in May 2027 for 3,999 euros. 
Anker’s Eufy brand includes its lineup of robot vacuums and robot lawn mowers, as well as home security products. At IFA Berlin 2026, the company revealed that after 14 years of operation, it’s bringing all of its sub-brands, including Eufy and Soundcore for the audio products, under the Anker umbrella. In the future, all Anker products, no matter if they’re robot vacuums, robot lawn mowers, security cameras, smart doorbells or headphones, will be sold under the Anker brand. 
The change isn’t immediate, and an Anker representative who briefed me prior to IFA said that for a time, consumers in different markets will still see the distinct branding across product categories, but the goal is to consolidate it under Anker. For now, the lineup announced at IFA 2026 will continue to use the Eufy or Soundcore branding. The announcement coincides with the opening of an official Anker Flagship store in Berlin on Sept. 5, the company’s first standalone retail store there. 
I cover vacuums and home tech for CNET. While I’m here at IFA Berlin 2026, I’m determined to seek out the most interesting tech that solves your most common cleaning problems. I’m excited to see the Eufy Omni E35 and Eufy S2 Max. I’ll report back with my impressions later.
Editors’ note: Ajay Kumar’s travel costs for the IFA conference were covered by Roborock. The judgments and opinions of CNET are our own.

66,2026-09-03,"The base Ioniq 3 doesn’t seem to offer an instrument cluster at all. 
Remember when iPhones used to come with free headphones and a phone charger (including the wall plug)? It didn’t feel so much like Apple giving you free goodies as it did that the company was providing you with the relevant hardware to use the device. Apple stopped including headphones and charging blocks in 2020. Now, Hyundai is pulling some of its standard hardware from the box, at least for two models.
Hyundai is charging customers extra for a driver’s display in the new Elantra generation (more specifically, the Korea-market Avante), as well as the Ioniq 3, Motor1 reported. Both models feature Pleos Connect, Hyundai’s new infotainment setup that pairs a center touchscreen with a slim 9.9-inch instrument cluster mounted above the dashboard—except where it doesn’t.
See all results for used Hyundai for sale near 85040
In its domestic market, the instrument cluster screen is offered as a 350,000 won ($255) option for the base trim. Not the most expensive optional extra in the world, but still kind of a slap in the face for a feature traditionally viewed as standard fare. Things are more expensive for the electric Ioniq 3. In the EV’s case, the base trim gives customers the full Tesla-screen experience, meaning if customers want the driver’s display, they’ll need to fork over the additional $5000 necessary to move up to the next-level trim.
The Pleos Connect setup initially launched on the more premium Grandeur sedan, though that model, with its higher base price, comes equipped with the additional screen in every trim. Based on Hyundai’s configurator, important functions like vehicle speed or any warning messages will be carried out via the central screen.
As things currently stand, no Hyundai, Kia, or Genesis products sold in the United States feature the Pleos Connect system, meaning customers don’t have to choose between saving a few bucks or seeing their speed directly in their sightlines. However, North American customers will have to make that decision soon, as Hyundai plans to have the setup equipped in 20 million cars globally by the end of the decade.

67,2026-09-03,"Palantir just posted the kind of revenue growth that makes software investors stop mid-scroll, yet the stock sits 8% below its all-time high with insiders selling and a valuation that has skeptics reaching for antacids. Whether that tension resolves as…
Read More: Learn 7 secret wealth tips high net worth investors use that most investors miss (sponsor)
This post may contain links from our sponsors and affiliates, and Flywheel Publishing may receive compensation for actions taken through them.

Palantir (NASDAQ:PLTR | PLTR Price Prediction) is closing in on a market cap of roughly $387 billion, and the trillion-dollar question is whether hyper-growth in U.S. commercial AI can justify the multiple. After a Q2 that CEO Alex Karp called “otherworldly”, our proprietary model still sees room to run.
The 24/7 Wall St. price target for Palantir is $219.66 over the next 12 months, an upside of 30.8% from the current $168.02. Our model signals meaningful upside with high confidence.
PLTR has been on a tear. Shares are up 46.21% over the past month and 4.16% over the past week, though today’s session opened weak with a 6.62% pullback from the prior close of $179.92. YTD, the stock is only 1.22% higher after a February drawdown, and it sits 8% below its 52-week high of $207.52.
The catalyst has been Q2 FY2026: revenue of $1.94 billion grew 92.83% year over year, EPS of $0.41 beat consensus by 46.43%, and U.S. commercial revenue rocketed 149%. Management raised full-year guidance to $8.150 to $8.158 billion, which they described as their “largest-ever full-year revenue guidance raise.”
Bulls have a straightforward thesis. Palantir’s Rule of 40 score hit 155%, net dollar retention reached 157%, and Total Contract Value grew 129% on a duration-weighted basis. Karp told investors he is “driving the business to grow at a rate equal or above to what we have in U.S. commercial for the next 18 months.”
Government exposure adds another leg: the FY2027 Department of War budget requests a historic $58.5 billion for AI investment, and Palantir’s trailing 12-month defense revenue is still less than 25 basis points of the Pentagon’s budget. Our bull-case one-year target is $228.06.
The bear case starts with valuation. PLTR trades at a trailing P/E of 255 and a price-to-free-cash-flow of 197. Insider activity has skewed toward selling, and Polymarket’s near-term composite sentiment reads bearish at 39.55.
Stock-based comp of $265 million in Q2 is real dilution. Bulls will note that SBC funds the elite technical hiring driving the platform, and GAAP operating margin still expanded to 47%. Our bear-case one-year target is $182.48.
ServiceNow (NYSE:NOW) is the closest scaled enterprise AI workflow peer. NOW posted Q2 FY2026 revenue of $3.99 billion, up 24%, with ServiceNow AI crossing $1 billion in ACV. Palantir grew nearly four times faster off a smaller base, which supports our premium target.
Snowflake (NYSE:SNOW) is the data-platform contrast. SNOW’s Q1 FY2027 revenue rose 33.5% to $1.39 billion, but the company remains unprofitable with a negative earnings yield of -1.2%. Palantir’s 62% adjusted operating margin makes the 24/7 Wall St. price target look reasonable relative to this peer set.
The 24/7 Wall St. price target of $219.66 rests on one factor above all: Palantir is compounding growth and margin simultaneously at a scale nothing else in software matches.
The constructive case strengthens if U.S. commercial TCV keeps growing above 100%. The setup weakens if the Rule of 40 score slips below 100% or if government AI budgets stall. For now, the fundamentals support the premium multiple.
These projections assume Palantir executes on its sovereign AI roadmap and defense pipeline. A recession-driven pause in enterprise AI budgets, or accelerated share dilution, could compress this trajectory materially.

This post may contain links from our sponsors and affiliates, and Flywheel Publishing may receive compensation for actions taken through them.
Robinhood Markets (NASDAQ:HOOD | HOOD Price Prediction) stock is rallying 11% to $118.88 in Thursday trading as a series of bullish analyst calls reinforces the case that Robinhood is becoming more than a traditional retail brokerage. Webull (NASDAQ:BULL) stock is climbing 7% to $9.62, while Interactive Brokers (NASDAQ:IBKR) stock is gaining 4% to $92.44, giving the broader online-brokerage group a strong showing.
Robinhood’s latest boost follows Morgan Stanley’s September 1 upgrade of Robinhood stock to Overweight from Equal Weight, accompanied by a price-target increase to $150 from $124. Morgan Stanley also raised its earnings estimates for Robinhood as analysts pointed to prediction markets, growing customer assets and the company’s expanding collection of financial products as additional sources of growth.
Robinhood’s second-quarter results provided plenty of evidence for the bullish argument, with revenue reaching a record $1.31 billion and net income climbing 48% year over year to $573 million. Robinhood Markets also reported 28.5 million funded customers and $355 billion in total platform assets as of July 31, while its 12-month net-deposit growth rate stood at 25%.
Robinhood’s prediction-market business is particularly important because event-contract revenue reached $156 million in Q2, surpassing cryptocurrency revenue of $100 million. Robinhood also reported that event contracts traded increased more than tenfold year over year to 13.6 billion, suggesting that prediction markets could become an increasingly important part of the platform’s economics.
Webull stock is climbing alongside Robinhood stock, although the two companies have different stages of growth and product development. Webull’s 7% advance gives investors another indication that enthusiasm around retail trading platforms is extending beyond HOOD, while the smaller brokerage remains more exposed to fluctuations in trading activity and investor sentiment.
Interactive Brokers stock is also gaining 4% to $92.44, although Interactive Brokers has a substantially different business mix that includes institutional customers and a broad international footprint. Interactive Brokers reported 5.19 million customer accounts in Q2, up 34% year over year, while average daily revenue trades increased 36% and margin loans jumped 67%.
The iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (NASDAQ:IBIT) is rising 2% to $44.65, while Bitcoin (CRYPTO:BTC) is up 3% over the past 24 hours to $78,830.12. That backdrop is helpful for Robinhood Markets because cryptocurrency remains an important part of its platform, even though the latest bullish thesis increasingly rests on prediction markets, active trading, asset-based revenue and other businesses.
Robinhood’s Q2 cryptocurrency trading volume actually declined 38% year over year, demonstrating why the company’s diversification matters. Robinhood Markets has also expanded into banking, retirement, credit cards, advisory services and prediction markets, giving the business several potential growth engines if cryptocurrency activity remains uneven.
The bullish case for Robinhood is becoming broader as the platform adds products that can increase activity and assets per customer. Morgan Stanley’s September upgrade reflects the view that Robinhood can generate more revenue from its existing customer base rather than depending primarily on adding new funded accounts.
The bearish case is that expectations are rising along with HOOD stock, potentially leaving less room for disappointment. Robinhood also remains exposed to trading volumes, cryptocurrency prices, prediction-market regulation and the possibility that some of its newer businesses won’t produce the economics investors currently anticipate.
Robinhood stock’s 11% Thursday-morning rally puts the shares close to the $120.05 technical buy point identified after the recent analyst upgrade. Investors can watch for whether HOOD stock can hold its gains while Robinhood continues converting its expanding product lineup into recurring revenue and customer assets.
Robinhood Markets has a stronger growth story than the traditional brokerage label might suggest, but the stock’s rapid advance also means expectations could become demanding. Investors who want exposure to the expansion of retail trading, prediction markets and digital financial services should consider keeping their HOOD position sizes moderate, particularly after an 11% single-day move.

Insider information is a great thing, especially in today's world. From insiders, we learn everything we need and don't need to know. From Charlie D'Amelio's personal breaking news to stock tickers, from the Lakers locker room to the White House.

In addition to established insiders who have made gossip their job and main source of income, there are also other types of insiders. These are just people who not only do their job, they are well versed in it - but at the same time, they know something about it that an ordinary person could hardly guess.
And, to be honest, we would hardly have thought of it, if not for this thread in the AskReddit community, whose topic starter asked both a simple and non-standard question: ""What are the facts about your job that general public has no idea about?"" To date, the thread has over 11.6K upvotes and almost 8.5K comments, where people willingly reveal all the light and dark sides of their work.
Bored Panda collected for you the most interesting, surprising and unusual revelations from representatives of various professions. Some, perhaps, you already knew well, and some will probably shock you. Either way, feel free to scroll to the very end of the post not to miss something really interesting. And now, a little inside info from our work - more than anything in this life, we love your upvotes and comments!
More info: Reddit
Click here & follow us for more lists, facts, and stories.

  That as a trucker, that space I left in front of me is so I don’t [end] anybody, NOT your personal invitation to jump in front of my bumper because you forgot your exit or whatever reason. So many want to get in front of us and slow down and park in front my bumper. YSK: Don’t believe the billboards those ambulance chasing lawyers put up about big truck accidents mean big bucks. Only survivors get money, most likely not you. If you do survive, more than likely your quality of life is going to be miserable. Also YSK: these trucks can weigh up to 80,000 pounds, 34,000 pounds empty and around 20,000 pounds without the trailer. How does that compare to your SUV or even your lifted pickup. Do us all a favor and give us some space, leave us room so that you can live and go home to your family One final thought, NEVER assume the other driver sees you. Wow! Thanks for all the upvotes. Glad you found it helpful.  
   gfinchster  ,   Robin Jakobsson    Report  
  Public defender. I don’t do this because it’s the only job I could get. I do it because I’m passionately invested in making sure poor people have access to the courts and because without us, there would be absolutely nothing between them and the awesome power of the government. The vast majority of us are there for the same reason. The perception that we’re not as good as private attorneys is just wrong. In fact, most PDs have done many more trials than privates because we have many more clients. The difference is that we can’t pick and choose our clients as private attorneys can. We have to work with what we get so our win/loss ratio isn’t as impressive as theirs. Ask a private defense attorney what they think of public defenders- most will tell you that some of the best attorneys they’ve known are PDs. While we can’t take the time to hold your hand and take every one of your calls, we actually do care about you and your case. I work nights. I work weekends. In fact, every weekend I’m at the jail, seeing my guys, because I don’t have time during the week. We are excellent attorneys who chose public service.

  When you call 911, please say your address before anything else. It doesn’t matter if someone is actively dying, say your address first. (I’m from a rural area so this might just be a problem where I’m from) but if you’re calling on a cell phone sometimes it can ping in a completely different spot then you are. If you call and don’t say where you are and we get disconnected, I might not have any clue where to send rescue/police/fire and therefore I cannot help you. Say your address (if you don’t know then please give like a cross street or notable location) first, then preferably your name, THEN tell me what’s going on. A lot of people assume that we automatically have a precise location where they’re at, and the systems can be pretty accurate but you can’t always rely on them. If you say the address and we get disconnected I can at least send someone to the area to figure out what needs to be done. (Source: I am a 911 dispatcher and I have so many people scream at me for not automatically knowing their location)

71,2026-09-03,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Kori X, who is based in Phoenix. They were originally laid off from Uber in 2023, returned this year as an evaluations specialist on the GenAI team, and were laid off again. Their words have been edited for length and clarity.
I was first laid off by Uber in 2023 after working my way up through multiple roles. When I woke up yesterday morning and saw the email that four months after I had rejoined the company, I was being laid off again, I wanted to throw my phone at the wall.
I was extremely nervous to rejoin Uber this year after already being laid off once. I used to love this company, but I was so afraid it would happen again. I needed a new job, so I talked to some of my friends who were still at Uber, and then in May, they helped me to rejoin as an evaluations specialist on the GenAI team.
Unfortunately, my fears became reality this week.
After my 2023 layoff, I ventured out and was a manager at a store for a minute, but unfortunately, that shop closed. So I thought, ""Okay, maybe it's time, give Uber another shot. I'll go back.""
Within weeks, I was rehired and working on a team of 12 to 15 people. I was constantly working in the office, too. I was also on an employee experience team. I was planning events for upcoming holidays, everything like that.
I was shocked when I received an email from my manager saying that they and other people on my team were also let go. To me, nothing alluded to this.
Last I heard, the company had just laid off a couple of people, maybe a month or so ago, and it seemed like we had a minute to not worry before they were going to do more.
My internal system access was cut off at 5 p.m. local time yesterday, but I wasn't able to get into Slack before then.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
I got 21 weeks of severance pay based on tenure and two months of ""garden leave,"" where I'll get my salary until November. Until then, I'll remain on payroll, with equity vesting and health benefits uninterrupted.
I'm not waiting until then to apply for jobs. I started looking as soon as I got the email. Unfortunately, the job market is really hard out there. I was just in it earlier this year.
I have to live somehow. If corporate is what it takes, then I'll unfortunately go back to corporate life. But if I can find a smaller shop or somewhere I can go that is not in a corporate environment, that's probably where I would prefer to be.
I don't agree with the CEO's assessment that there were too many management layers. And if there were, it's his fault. That's all done by upper leadership. That has nothing to do with frontline workers.
I think these are excuses for why Uber wanted to cut people, but I don't think it has anything to do with management or too much overlap. I think they're seeing that AI products can replace a lot of that, and they can make more money with fewer people.
If they're going to lay off people, maybe be a little bit more discerning as to who you're laying off, because there were really good people that were there for a really long time who were affected. I was there for years before my first layoff. I knew that place like the back of my hand, and they didn't care.
Uber did not respond to a request for comment.
Do you have a story to share about a recent layoff? If so, please reach out to the reporter at aapplegate@businessinsider.com.

73,2026-09-03,"Olivia Tai stood in a San Jose mall with her bright red sign, proclaiming: ""Everyone has an AI secret.""
Two tween girls approached, arms full of shopping bags. They quickly scribbled down their confessions, chatting with Tai in their Valley Girl affect. Tai asked about how they used AI at school.
""Everyone uses AI, and if you don't use it, you're an overachiever,"" the girls told her.
Tai wasn't just fishing for tech secrets. The 26-year-old from San Francisco was developing her art project, called ""What We Tell AI."" She asked strangers across the country to leave anonymous notes about their AI-related shames — and then displayed them on a website.
Americans are forming increasingly personal relationships with AI. A September survey from Elon University and YouGov found that 27% of US adult internet users had ""significant emotional or social interactions"" with AI.
Tai saw this first hand. The project turned her into something like a radio host accepting late-night calls, she said. Friends told her about their ChatGPT-written wedding vows; strangers told her they lied to their managers about AI.
""I didn't know how this project would impact people,"" she said. ""What I'm learning is that, sometimes, the poignancy of a single, really clear story in the form of a note can actually spread way more dialogue.""
Tai was tired of people turning into ""debate kids"" when they talked about AI.
In the summer of 2025, she hosted a series of workshops in New York and San Francisco about AI's impact on the world. She noticed that people would get too abstract or think too far into the future.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
So, she introduced the confessions. At the start of the workshop, she'd ask attendees to write down a secret about their relationship with AI. They'd put the notes in a bowl, mix them up, redistribute them, and have everyone read the admissions aloud.
""It makes the stakes really personal,"" she said. ""It makes things real.""
After a few workshops, she thought the confessions would make a good art project. She set up a prototype in San Francisco's Dolores Park, with a cardboard box and a Sharpie-written sign.
One note that day, in particular, struck her. It was about cloning an ex-boyfriend's voice to ""have a last connection with him."" The AI cloning ""helped me finally move on,"" the note read.
Tai found it ""poignant"" and ""strange"" — and decided to turn it into a proper art project in multiple cities.
She painted a wooden sign bright red. ""It's meant to be provocative,"" Tai said. She'd load it into her granny cart, along with an instruction folder and a picture frame containing six example notes.
Not everyone got it. Some would leave confessions about long-lost loves with no AI relation. Others would leave prank notes. Still, she ended up with a trove of thoughtful responses.
After collecting the notes, Tai kept them in little plastic bags, organized by where they were written. She then uploaded them to her site, designed by her friend Arin Pantja.
Tai is still scanning the notes, so the site is being updated continually and could continue to grow. People are asking Tai if they can contribute and complete the experiment in Mexico, Australia, and China, she said.
Tai called the project a ""snapshot"" of San Francisco.
It's not that she only collected data from the city; she hopped between San Francisco, San Jose, and New York. Rather, it's about the spirit of the confessors.
""You won't come up to the booth if you don't actively have a relationship with AI,"" she said. ""I'm in a lot of spaces that are going to have early adopters.""
Of the cities, San Francisco had the boldest responses. In New York's Luddite community, it's shameful to say you wrote an email with AI, Tai said. In the Bay Area, that's table stakes.
Some of the San Francisco confessors wondered if she was doing market research for an AI company. ""People who work in AI would be scared, and a little bit suspicious,"" Tai said.
She compared it to New York, where people worried she was a content creator, filming them as they wrote.
Tai calls herself an ""artist-researcher."" She later clarifies that she more identifies with the artist title, but that the process of collecting notes felt like an ""anthropological, ethnographic snapshot.""
She isn't an expert, Tai said, and she's not telling people to ""have some takeaway."" Rather, she's asking people to reflect more deeply on the ""slippery slope"" of AI.
""There are all these new norms coming about,"" Tai said. ""Which ones do we actually want?""
No comments right now, check back later.
Comments are unavailable right now.

74,2026-09-03,"This story is based on an interview with Angela Cheng-Cimini, 56, a veteran HR professional who lives near Baltimore. It has been edited for length and clarity.
A few years ago, my husband, Michael, and I started losing friends who were around our age.
Three, in particular, died of pancreatic cancer, which was very sudden. Those kinds of things really stop you short. It's an aberration when you're in your 30s, but it becomes more common in your 50s.
The natural reaction is to consider your own mortality. I'd been working full steam ahead for 30 years, across everything from start-ups to mature companies, and needed to reassess.
Have you taken a step back in your job or profession? Please email Jane Ridley at jridley@businessinsider.com to share your story with Business Insider.
I was the chief HR officer at Harvard Business Publishing at the time, a role that was extremely fulfilling. However, I didn't know how to compartmentalize. I bought work home psychologically, emotionally, and, to some degree, physically.
I was ignoring self-care. The universe was telling me that it was time for a change. So, after a lot of deliberation, I resigned in December 2024.
Some people might have regarded it as early retirement, but I called it ""rewirement."" I wasn't ready to go the full nine yards and quit my career, so I began looking for flexible-schedule opportunities.
A Google search led me to the organization, The Chronicle of Philanthropy, a media and information resource for the nonprofit sector.
I'd intended to take several months off, just to relax. But its part-time chief HR officer position stood out to me.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
I've been in the job since January 2025, working three days a week while receiving health insurance, 401(k) contribution, and paid time off.
It's a win-win: I get to stay active in my profession, while my small employer has access to my three decades of experience at a fraction of the cost of a regular salary.
The arrangement is perfect for someone my age because I keep my toes in the industry, and it benefits from my wisdom.
I mentor not only colleagues but also people outside my organization. The extra time I have off enables me to share my knowledge with a younger generation of professionals who often reach out to me on LinkedIn.
When I was coming up in my career, there were hardly any female Asian leaders that I could model myself after. I felt that I should pay it back by paying it forward.
I mentored in the past, but it often felt like I was shoe-horning it in between other responsibilities. Now I can easily volunteer 45 minutes on a call.
I'm also on several advisory boards for startup organizations in the HR space, helping entrepreneurs assess the market fit of their solutions. It's all unpaid and very gratifying.
Meanwhile, Michael, who is retired, and I make the most of our leisure time. We love traveling and recently went on a bucket-list trip down the Nile aboard a 100-year-old steamship.
I'm planning to stay in my job for as long as they'll have me. Still, I'm conscious that I have fewer years ahead than behind.
Wednesday afternoon comes along, and I'm ready to shut down my laptop after three days of work that stimulates my brain.
Then I can prioritize the other things that are important to me. My ""rewirement"" has changed my whole outlook on life.

75,2026-09-03,"As an only child, an occasional weekend slumber party was the longest I'd had to share a bedroom or bathroom — or anything, really — until I was a freshman in college.
So last year, when my 62-year-old mom proposed putting her house on the market and us buying a place together, I was incredulous — I had no idea how that arrangement would work for us. Even though we'd had nearly 20 years of cohabitation experience while I was growing up, it was hard to picture us as natural roommates.
Needless to say, our decision to move in together was not without its challenges, but it was the smartest choice we've made for both of our futures.
Pooling our resources to buy this house and split the bills has proven to be a highly prudent financial decision for both of us. As a 28-year-old, I harbored no fantasies that I'd be able to afford even a small condo or townhouse on my own within the next 10 years.
Now that I share a 1,500-square-foot house with my mom, I pay less in monthly rent toward our mortgage than I did in my previous, 900-square-foot apartment.
My mom gets to pay a lot less, too, which affords us an even greater opportunity to tailor the house to our needs. She's already added her work studio, and I'm designing a garden.
Even though it's been an easier financial load, the day-to-day dynamics of living together are still sometimes challenging.
For instance, my mother believes there is exactly one space in the house appropriate for enjoying a meal. I think the dried pasta shell I recently dug out from between the cushions of my beloved sofa adequately illustrates my argument to the contrary.
Additionally, my mom still finds beauty in the austerity of a bare wall, where I see a blank canvas ripe for design.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
When we do argue, it's been relatively petty, and usually about the best way to handle the ongoing afternoon war games between her dog, our ancient family Shih Tzu named Aggi, and my cat, Gravel.
Luckily, to my mother's credit, she knows she's not living with the teenage version of me anymore, so she doesn't try to impose a curfew or check if I'm keeping my room clean, thankfully.
The same is true of my dating life. My partner and I started seeing each other around the time my mother and I decided to move in together, and even though it's required some negotiation, boundary-setting, and patience, it's overall been great.
Plus, it's helped bring my partner and me closer together and enabled us to develop a shared understanding of each other's priorities, intentions, and future commitments.
Don't get me wrong, living independently for the past 10 years was a delight, if only for the pure satisfaction of knowing mine were the only grubby fingers rummaging around in the bag of shredded Mexican cheese.
However, I look forward to having children one day, and as someone who spent a lot of time with her grandmother growing up, I would love for my kids to have that same experience.
The daily interactions I had with my grandparents inspired my lifelong love of knitting, watching ""The Golden Girls,"" gardening, and gossiping, which are still among my favorite hobbies.
Living with my mother will offer my future children that kind of valuable exposure, not to mention the tremendous relief of having a live-in parental pressure valve.
My mom was also thinking about her future when we made this decision. At 62, the countdown to her retirement gets louder every year, and as a lifelong nomad, she's been looking forward to resuming her world travels on a more full-time basis — with our house serving as her well-maintained, low-cost home base.
Additionally, as my mother's only child, she and I have had several frank conversations about expectations and responsibilities as she ages.
Living together now offers us both greater peace of mind and will hopefully prevent us from being unpleasantly surprised if or when the day comes that she can no longer live independently.
Overall, I've actually been pleasantly surprised by how well my mom and I have been able to coexist under one roof and find a middle path by reminding ourselves of the reasons we bought the house.
And when that doesn't work, we have ample opportunities for escape: a breezy front porch, our own distinct work spaces, and, critically, bedrooms on different floors.

NYPD officials are describing this summer as one of the safest ever in New York City history. But that overall trend comes with caveats — as reports of felony assaults continue to rise slightly, and hate crimes are up 15% year-to-date.
Shootings, murders, robberies and burglaries all continued declining month-over-month in August, as they have all year, including in the city's public housing complexes and subway systems. Experts say June, July and August tend to be an important telltale for crime analysts and public safety officials because of a surge in violence that’s fueled in part by more late-night, outdoor gatherings that involve alcohol.
NYPD Commissioner Jessica Tisch touted that the stats were achieved as the department managed “an unprecedented series of high-profile events,” including the Knicks' NBA Championship, the World Cup, Sail 250, and America’s 250th Fourth of July celebration. She attributed some of the progress to the department’s Summer Violence Reduction Plan, which launched in May and deployed officers at the specific times and places where violence tends to concentrate.
“It's been a great summer,” said Christopher Herrmann, a former NYPD crime analyst who is now a professor at CUNY’s John Jay College of Criminal Justice. He said he’s “very surprised” at how prevalent the declines have been.
“Typically we would see decreases in boroughs, maybe even the entire borough. But very rarely do you get a citywide decline with very few increases at the neighborhood level,” he said.
The fact that the crime spike didn’t really materialize this summer could be the result of a combination of factors, Herrmann said — including precision policing and the NYPD’s ability to allocate more officers to the places that might need them.
In the Bronx, where NYPD Commissioner Tisch has announced plans to send 200 more officers to address pervasive public safety issues, reported shootings and murders were also down this August compared to August 2025. Reported murders dropped by about 36%, and reported shooting incidents dropped by about 22%.
In Brooklyn, the continued decline in violence means the borough is on track to report another record-low year for crime, according to District Attorney Eric Gonzalez. Over June, July and August of 2025, the borough recorded 28 homicides; this year, it recorded 15.
Gonzalez said the decreases have allowed his office to “ really focus our efforts on those folks who do pick up guns and pull triggers.” He said his office is investing in on-the-ground anti-violence organizations and improving technologies for presenting evidence in court to make prosecution more efficient
According to Herrmann and the Council on Criminal Justice, a national organization focused on criminal justice policy, there are also external forces at play: the entire country is experiencing a decline in crime this year, and New York City is following that trend.
Research specialist Ernesto Lopez, one of the authors of the Council on Criminal Justice's most recent report, said the country is on track to experience its lowest homicide rate since 1900. Possible factors for that pattern include ""larger social changes” like an aging demographic, more young people spending time alone and on their phones, improved policing technologies and increased investment in local violence intervention programs.
In the midst of all the declines, experts are still trying to understand why reported felony assaults — defined as the kinds of attacks that leave people with serious injuries — are still on the rise. NYPD officials said felony assaults have increased by around 3% this August compared to the same month last year, with more than 2,660 incidents reported. However, felony assaults are only up less than 1% year-to-date, compared to 2025.
“Typically, when violence falls, violence falls, but in this case, we're starting to see an increase in assault,” Herrmann said. “And I'm kind of curious, is this going to be the start of the other violent crimes now starting to reverse that trend, starting to increase as well?”
According to NYPD data, reoffenders are a bigger problem in the felony assault category than they have been in years past. By this time in 2018, police arrested fewer than 100 people for allegedly committing multiple felony assaults. In 2026, officials said, the number of such alleged three-time reoffenders has almost tripled.
""The NYPD has served its role in arresting and going after felony assault cases with nearly 80% of these cases resulting in arrests,” Brad Weekes, an NYPD spokesperson, said. “We believe that we are playing our part in the criminal justice system.""
Reports of hate crimes are also on the rise, with an increase of more than 15% so far this year, compared to the same period last year. The spike has been fueled partly by anti-Jewish hate crimes, which alone accounted for more than half of the confirmed hate crimes for the first eight months of the year — even though Jewish New Yorkers only account for 10% of the city’s population — according to NYPD data.
Herrmann said some of that could be attributed to the conflicts in the Middle East, but also to the fact that victims are more aware of the issue and therefore more likely to report it.
Where crime is rising
Despite the positive trends, felony assaults in particular can lead to the perception among residents of imminent danger, especially when they appear to be random, Herrmann said. This week’s fatal stabbing in Times Square — which killed 32-year-old Erin Piacenti as she left work, left another man injured, and left the perpetrator dead after an encounter with police — is exactly the kind of thing that stokes fear, he added.
“There's nothing more rattling for public safety,” he said. “The reality is none of us when we're leaving our office buildings or schools or whatever, we're not necessarily ready for someone who is going to just attack us out of the blue, you know?”
On a borough-wide level, Gonzalez said, residents’ perception of safety is finally starting to catch up with the stats.
“Overwhelmingly, people are telling me they feel safer than they felt before. They're starting to believe the numbers. They're starting to feel it,” he said. “I can tell you just a few years ago, people told me the opposite.”
Now that the crime spikes of the COVID-19 pandemic appear to be behind New York City, Herrmann said, the big question among experts is how much lower it can go.
“We're starting to wonder about the current trend,” he said. “When is it going to level off? And then even when is it going to start to go back up again?”https://counciloncj.org/crime-trends-in-u-s-cities-mid-year-2026-update/
